Stanford Physicist Uses AI to Tackle Quantum Gravity Problem
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:55:57 UTC
23:55:57 KST

Stanford Physicist Uses AI to Tackle Quantum Gravity Problem
Insider Brief
- Stanford physicist Venkatesa Chandrasekaran proposed a quantum theory of gravity that uses the paths of light to calculate how spacetime can fluctuate.
- The approach yielded a finite result for a graviton-pair calculation that diverges under an approximation and reproduced a known quantum correction to black hole entropy.
- Chandrasekaran said he developed several calculations and proofs through months of work with OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra, using about 400 million tokens.
A Stanford physicist has proposed a quantum theory of gravity that uses the paths of light to get a finite answer for a calculation that can otherwise run to infinity.
If the approach holds up, it could help reconcile quantum mechanics, which governs the behavior of matter and energy at small scales, with Einstein’s general relativity, which describes gravity as the curvature of spacetime. The study also reproduces a known quantum correction to the entropy of a black hole.
In a preprint posted on arXiv, Venkatesa Chandrasekaran calculated what happens when two gravitons, the hypothetical quantum units of gravitational waves, are created and then disappear. The calculation gave a finite answer even after accounting for waves of arbitrarily short wavelength. An approximation applied to the same calculation produced an infinity.
Chandrasekaran said in the paper’s acknowledgments that he developed several of its calculations and proofs through months of back-and-forth work with GPT-6 Astra, an OpenAI model. He put the total at about 400 million tokens, the units of text AI models process, and said OpenAI provided the computing resources.
The work addresses a problem of reconciling general relativity with quantum theory, a problem that, as mentioned, has vexed top physicists for decades. General relativity treats gravity as the curvature of spacetime. In plain terms, quantum theory allows physical quantities to fluctuate. If spacetime itself fluctuates, the geometry that ordinarily tells physicists where and when something happens also becomes part of what they have to calculate.
Chandrasekaran’s proposal uses the boundaries of finite regions of spacetime to set up those calculations. The boundaries follow paths that light could take, and their shape can change with the gravitational field. That gives the calculation a way to specify where something happens even as the geometry changes.
The study also finds that a region bounded this way, with a specified maximum size, can hold only a finite number of mutually distinguishable quantum states. In a separate calculation for a large black hole, the approach recovered both the standard relationship between a black hole’s entropy and its surface area and a previously calculated quantum correction.
It’s important to note that the paper is theoretical and presents no experimental test.
Using Light to Set the Boundaries
To calculate how gravity behaves when spacetime itself can fluctuate, Chandrasekaran starts by defining the region he wants to study. He uses the paths light can take between two events to set its boundaries.
One way to envision this is to imagine a flash going off and a detector set to record light at a particular moment later. Light spreading out from the flash marks one boundary. Tracing the possible paths of light backward from the detector at that moment marks the other. The two boundaries enclose a region called a causal diamond. Any point inside it can receive a signal from the flash and send one in time to reach the detector.
In general relativity, gravitational waves can distort those boundaries. The paper treats the surface where they meet as part of the system and locates quantities relative to it. Chandrasekaran first solves the classical equations that constrain which combinations of boundary data are possible. He then applies quantum rules to the remaining variables, which include the two ways a gravitational wave can be polarized.
To describe a larger stretch of spacetime, the paper joins regions along shared boundaries. It matches the gravitational data on the two sides and sums over the quantum states that can occur at an intermediate stage. Chandrasekaran shows that these intermediate sums converge within the construction. In fact, they converge even when the set of possible states is infinite.
The order of operations matters, as the graviton-pair calculation shows.
In practice, physicists often start with a rough answer and refine it by adding corrections based on the strength of the interaction.
In this example, expanding in Newton’s gravitational constant before adding the contributions from different wavelengths produces a term that grows without bound. Keeping the constant at its physical, nonzero value and doing the sum first gives a finite answer. At very short wavelengths, contributions that would otherwise drive the divergence partly cancel.
Counting States Inside a Black Hole
For the state-counting result, Chandrasekaran imposes a maximum size on a macroscopic causal diamond using what he calls a gravitational clock. The clock is a distance-like reading defined along the paths of light. Gravitational radiation changes that reading by affecting how neighboring light paths converge. Putting an upper limit on the reading also restricts which wave states can fit within the region.
The study concludes that a causal diamond with a fixed size limit can contain only a finite number of distinct quantum states. That means the area where the two light cones meet can take only certain values, with gaps between them.
The black hole calculation uses a surface where a bundle of outward-directed light rays momentarily stops spreading. The local gravitational data are enough to identify that surface and begin counting states on its interior side.
By summing over interior states, Chandrasekaran recovers the leading term in the Bekenstein-Hawking formula, which makes a black hole’s entropy proportional to its horizon area. Entropy describes how much microscopic information is compatible with the same large-scale description.
For a large, nonrotating black hole, the study also found a small adjustment to the standard entropy formula that grows slowly as the black hole’s surface area increases. The result matches an earlier calculation by physicist Ashoke Sen that held the black hole’s mass fixed and accounted for small variations in its spin.
Next Steps
A lot of work remains.
The four-dimensional theory covers gravity in a vacuum, where gravitational waves can propagate. Extending it to include matter would widen its scope. The calculation also recovers Einstein’s equations in the low-energy limit for initial quantum states concentrated around a classical geometry. It calculates the first quantum correction in that regime, including how fluctuations in gravity in turn affect the average geometry.
One question Chandrasekaran leaves for future work is whether different choices in building the quantum state space lead to equivalent theories for the same range of clock readings. Another test would be to carry the black hole and low-energy calculations beyond the first quantum correction.
For a deeper, more technical dive, please review the paper on arXiv. It’s important to note that arXiv is a pre-print server, which allows researchers to receive quick feedback on their work. However, it is not — nor is this article, itself — official peer-review publications. Peer-review is an important step in the scientific process to verify results.
Keep track of everything going on in the Quantum Technology Market. In one place.
Topics
Matt Swayne
Get the latest research, company news, and market intelligence every week.

Show HN: I restored Palm's last device, and brought its OS to other platforms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:37:02 UTC
23:37:02 KST

In socializing recent innovations for the platform, I began to realize that there are people out there who don’t really know what webOS was. I guess I’ve lived with it so long that it never occurred to me that the rest of the world has moved on. While I had intended to write a post about the future for the platform, maybe a little primer on the past is in order – for the more casual explorer…
Long before the iPhone, the Palm Pilot was the “it” gadget that everyone wanted to have. Palm’s own OS made these devices useful and responsive, but by 2004, both the company and its platform were in a tough place. Efforts began to re-imagine both, and a completely new “web OS” was created to be contemporary of the original iPhone.
Under Apple alum Jon Rubinstein , Palm attracted some of the best designers and developers (many even from Apple), to create a fluid and beautiful new experience on limited hardware, by leveraging open technologies, like Linux, WebKit and JavaScript. The original Palm Pre, with its slide-out keyboard, was followed up by the Pre 2 and Pixi while they were still Palm, but the valiant efforts of the team were not enough to take on Google and Apple. They needed a new owner, with a bigger wallet.
HP decided they wanted to be that owner, and acqui-hired the Palm team . Work resumed on the diminutive Veer slide-out, the impressive Pre3, and the TouchPad tablet line under HP’s brand. The leadership promised webOS everywhere, and it looked like there might be a chance. The TouchPad launched against the iPad, with an updated version of webOS, innovative features that wouldn’t be copied by others for years, and a small but useful App Catalog… and then disaster struck.
HP had a leadership change, and foolishly decided to write off the Palm purchase . The dev team worked quickly to spin off a fully Open Source derivative, Open webOS , and accelerated efforts to make the SDK libraries usable cross-platform. The TouchPad went on fire sale , and most of the Pre3s never made it to market. The little group of innovators who dared to take on the Apple/Google Duopoly were defeated.
LG bought what was left of the commercial OS, and moved quickly to make it the platform for their Smart TVs. Other experiments, including a Smart Watch, only briefly glimpsed daylight. The Palm brand moved to TCL, and HP moved on to selling crappy Android and Windows 8 tablets. It seemed like the end…
But the famously active and dedicated Community weren’t willing to give up. webOS had been defined by Homebrew and the hacker ethos . Community members archived as much of the App Catalog as they could salvage before it was shut off. Open webOS was promptly picked up and became LuneOS – a community re-write that worked on the TouchPad and could be ported to other hardware. The TouchPad itself got some updates through Luna Community Edition, providing launcher features Palm never got to deliver.
Over the ensuing decade, though, the small contributions of the community were not enough to fight back the tide of progress. The ancient Linux kernel, WebKit and OpenSSH stacks became a challenge for the modern web. Previously open APIs that enabled apps and features got closed off as Meta and Google consolidated their iron-fisted control over our screen time. And app devs moved on to more profitable platforms.
In 2018, shortly before the world got shut down by Covid, I started work on restoring the SDK . I liked the fact that my TouchPad and Pre3 couldn’t do much – that meant Google wasn’t tracking my every move. It became a challenge to find a way to proxy, re-route, or update calls so I could keep apps alive, or restore them to functionality.
Community members with app archives, and a partially implemented App Museum, were quick to engage in my novice efforts, and work began in earnest to restore what we could. Coordinating on the (now dead, but archived) forums , and in occasional Zoom meetings, we collected our preserved bits into the “webOS Archive.” A new version of the App Museum made downloads “just work”, and I poured over repositories and zip files and questionable MegaUpload links to link together binaries with their scraped meta data.
Then a respiratory virus locked us all indoors, and I really started coding. Publishing as either webOS Archive (for platform-wide features) or codepoet (for hacks and restorations), I began building new functionality again. With a working app delivery mechanism, we started to get an idea of how many people were interested. I committed to making sure the platform was never as creepy as Google or Meta, but anonymous stats, opt-in features, and self-identifying users on our Discord server, gave us a picture of a small, but very real community still using their devices.
Still, it was a tough road. The move to TLS 1.3 increasingly made it difficult to do anything online, and while I hosted a SSL-bump proxy that made anonymous access possible (but insecure), actually logging-in to anything was also limited by so called “modern sign-in” like OAuth2 flows, that couldn’t render in the ancient browser. By 2024, I was about to give up…
Then two things happened, almost at once:
- Out of the LuneOS effort, long-time community member Herrie gave us an OpenSSH update.
- AI assisted coding got good – and it had in its training data all the documentation I’d built and restored for the platform.
There’s lots bad to be said about AI, and in my personal life, I’m conflicted about it. But the fact that Claude knows my passion project as well as I do has been an incredible accelerator. A decades worth of ideas started coming together in weeks or even days. The foundation we’d preserved, and the tools we’d put together, made it possible to do new things with our 15-year old platform.
While Palm’s phones died a different death with the 3G shut downs, WiFi-enabled devices, and particularly the TouchPad – which was remarkably powerful for its time – sprung back to life, as problems were knocked down one-by-one.
The Pre3 got a modernization suite that largely brought its original functionality (sans the mobile carriers) back online. The TouchPad got all that and more. This past summer I released webOS Community Edition 3.1.0 , replacing HP’s final system image (usually 3.0.5) with a firmware bundle including all of the Community’s additions, updates and fixes. Using that as a foundation, I started work on the last still-broken piece: the update server.
With Claude’s help, we reverse-engineered the signed and secure communication between the device and Palm/HP’s servers. A new key, included in 3.1.0, made sure that TouchPad’s would trust community server’s instead, and the new OS baseline made it possible to compute a delta update between the initial release and a bundle of updates.
webOS Community Edition 3.2.0 is available as flashable firmware OR as an Over the Air Update to TouchPad users running 3.1.0. Every non-commercial part of the platform has an open source, community run equivalent. webOS, as originally envisioned by Palm, lives on!
But even with bit-rot addressed through community software. The reality remains that the TouchPad, while the last and best Palm device, is showing its age. The CPU is slow, the RAM ceiling low, and the WiFi chip can’t connect to WiFi 6 and newer, faster networks. We need a way to run webOS on other hardware.
Lunacy for Android , and LuneOS from webOS Ports, are that way forward. In Part 2, I’ll talk more about how the community can engage in those efforts…

====================================================================================================

15 Leftover Turkey Recipes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

Got a mountain of leftover turkey sitting in the fridge and no idea what to make with it? After spending all that time (and money) on the big Thanksgiving meal, I want to make sure those leftovers don’t go to waste. These easy leftover turkey recipes include everything from a cozy pie and hearty soups to quick lunches and clever chicken-to-turkey swaps, so you can get a few more delicious meals out of that holiday bird!
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
Hearty Mains
The last thing I want to do after cooking a big Thanksgiving feast is start another complicated dinner from scratch. These hearty meals put that cooked turkey to work alongside affordable ingredients like beans, rice, potatoes, and vegetables. You’ll get a completely different dinner out of it, and maybe even enough for lunch the next day!
1. Turkey Pot Pie
Turkey Pot Pie Recipe
My turkey pot pie is one recipe where I happily skip making pastry from scratch! Ready-made pie crusts save time, and the creamy filling is packed with shredded turkey, potatoes, frozen mixed vegetables, and a little Parmesan for extra flavor. If you’ve made turkey broth from your Thanksgiving bird, you can use that in the filling, too.
2. Turkey Chili
Turkey Chili
Had enough of Thanksgiving flavors for a while? This hearty turkey chili is just what I’d put on the menu next. It’s loaded with three types of beans, sweet corn, poblano peppers, and warming spices that give the turkey a completely different flavor. The recipe usually starts by browning ground turkey, but with leftovers, I’d skip that step and stir in 2-3 cups of chopped cooked turkey toward the end of simmering. Or if you’ve got ground turkey in the fridge, check out our ground turkey recipes roundup for more dinner ideas!
3. Turkey and Wild Rice Soup
Turkey and Wild Rice Soup
If you’ve got a few carrots, some celery, and leftover turkey in the fridge, you’re already well on your way to a big pot of turkey and wild rice soup. Mushrooms, herbs, and a splash of cream give the broth plenty of flavor, while the rice makes it filling enough for dinner. I’d stick with a wild rice blend rather than buying pure wild rice. It’s generally cheaper, and the different grains give the soup a lovely mix of textures.
4. Thanksgiving Bowls
Thanksgiving Salad Bowl
I love a big, colorful salad after the holidays, especially when I can use up all those little bits and pieces taking up space in the fridge. My Thanksgiving bowls combine fresh greens, rice, roasted vegetables, crunchy apples, dried cranberries, and a homemade maple cinnamon dressing. Shredded leftover turkey makes a tasty addition, and I’d absolutely use any roasted sweet potatoes or Brussels sprouts already in the fridge rather than cooking more!
Lunches and Light Bites
You don’t always need another full dinner to get through those leftovers. Sometimes a handful of turkey is all it takes to make a satisfying lunch or something to share while everyone’s still relaxing after the holiday.
5. Turkey Sliders
Turkey Sliders Recipe
Turkey sandwiches are practically a Thanksgiving tradition, but these turkey sliders take things a little further. Soft Hawaiian rolls are layered with cooked turkey, cranberry sauce, and Swiss cheese, then brushed with a buttery Dijon and everything bagel seasoning glaze before baking. The cheese gets lovely and melty, and the tops turn golden in the oven. You can swap the Swiss for leftover cheese from your holiday cheese board, too!
6. Turkey Taco Salad
Turkey Taco Salad
For something fresh, crunchy, and completely different from a roast dinner, try this turkey taco salad. The recipe originally uses ground turkey, but I’d chop up some leftover roasted turkey and warm it in a skillet with the taco seasoning and a splash of water instead! You can also swap the homemade avocado dressing for whatever creamy dressing you’ve got in the fridge to save a little extra prep.
7. Thanksgiving Leftovers Tortilla Wrap
Thanksgiving Leftovers Tortilla Wrap
You know those containers in the fridge with barely enough leftovers for one serving? This Thanksgiving leftovers tortilla wrap is exactly what they’re good for. It uses just ¼ cup each of turkey, stuffing, and cranberry sauce, along with some creamy Brie, all folded into a large tortilla and toasted until golden. I’d chop the turkey into small pieces before assembling, so the wrap folds neatly and holds together.
8. Thanksgiving Potato Skins
Thanksgiving Potato Skins
After all the cooking that goes into Thanksgiving, I’m usually ready to hang up my apron for a few days. That’s part of the reason I love these Thanksgiving potato skins. I just scoop out baked potatoes and mash it with butter and sour cream, then fold in leftover turkey, stuffing, and peas before piling everything back into the skins. A little cranberry sauce, melted cheddar, and warm gravy for dipping finish them off. 😋
9. Leftover Stuffing Muffins
Leftover Stuffing Muffins
Leftover stuffing makes a surprisingly handy base for savory breakfast muffins! These individual portions combine it with cooked turkey, spinach, eggs, and milk, so they’re just as useful for a quick lunch or snack. These leftover stuffing muffins bake in a muffin pan, so there’s no slicing or portioning to worry about afterward. I’d also throw in a little shredded cheese or some finely chopped roasted vegetables if there are any left.
10. Holiday Leftovers Wreath
Holiday Leftovers Wreath
This holiday leftovers wreath is a real showstopper, and most of the filling is already sitting in your fridge! Turkey, mashed potatoes, stuffing, cranberry sauce, and mozzarella are wrapped inside buttery crescent roll dough, then baked into a golden wreath. Serve it with warm gravy in the center for dipping, and you’ve got something that looks like you spent far longer making it than you actually did.
Easy Chicken-to-Turkey Swaps
Cooked shredded turkey works really well in dishes that usually call for chicken, particularly when there’s plenty of sauce, cheese, or seasoning involved. I’ve included a few easy swaps below, along with any adjustments I’d make to avoid cooking the turkey longer than necessary.
11. Traditional Chicken Salad
Chicken Salad (Traditional)
This traditional chicken salad is one of the recipes I always make when my dad visits (and it’s just as easy to make with leftover turkey!) Replace the four cups of shredded rotisserie chicken with cooked turkey, then mix it with crunchy celery, red onion, juicy grapes, walnuts, and the creamy mayo dressing. There’s no cooking involved, and you can serve it in sandwiches, lettuce cups, or with crackers for an easy lunch.
12. Cheesy Chicken Nachos
Chicken Nachos
Crispy tortilla chips, plenty of melted cheese, seasoned turkey, and all your favorite toppings. What’s not to like? These chicken nachos are made with chicken breast in the original recipe, but there’s no reason you can’t use diced leftover turkey. I’d toss the cooked meat with taco seasoning and warm it through with a splash of water before layering it with the chips and cheese. Add the avocado, jalapeños, green onions, and other fresh toppings after baking so they stay fresh and crunchy. A tray of these would disappear pretty quickly at a family movie night!
13. Chicken Enchiladas
Chicken Enchiladas Recipe
Our chicken enchiladas already call for cooked shredded chicken, which makes them one of the easiest recipes to turn into a leftover turkey dinner. Simply replace the chicken with three cups of shredded turkey, mix with the cumin, garlic powder, and green chiles, then roll everything into flour tortillas. They’re covered in homemade red enchilada sauce and plenty of cheese before baking until bubbly.
14. Creamy White Chicken Lasagna
White Chicken Lasagna Recipe
When there’s a generous amount of turkey left in the fridge, I’d use it to make this creamy white chicken lasagna for my family. Layers of pasta, ricotta, spinach, mozzarella, Parmesan, and a rich homemade white sauce make it a comforting change from the usual tomato-based lasagna. It makes nine hearty portions, but you could cut it into twelve smaller servings and add a simple side salad to stretch it further!
15. White Chicken Chili
Creamy White Chicken Chili
Here’s another cozy dinner that would make excellent use of already-cooked turkey. This white chicken chili is packed with beans, green chiles, corn, and warming spices, with cream cheese and sour cream giving it a thick, creamy finish. Instead of simmering raw chicken thighs as the recipe directs, I’d cook the bean and vegetable base first, then add about three cups of shredded turkey along with the corn near the end. So easy, cheap, and perfect for using up leftover turkey!

Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:09:44 UTC
23:09:44 KST

Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse
The Norwegian low cost carrier Norse Atlantic Airways has suspended all flights as it rushes to resolve what it warned is a critical financial situation.
The airline said if no solution can be found, it intends to file for bankruptcy.
It uses a fleet of 12 Boeing 787 aircraft on long-haul routes from a number of European airports, including London Gatwick, Rome and Oslo.
Prior to the suspension of flights, it was advertising services from London to Orlando, Cape Town, Bangkok and Phuket.
Flight disruption
One aircraft was meant to depart London for Orlando this morning, while another was meant to arrive from Cape Town this evening.
The suspension has also affected Pakistan International Airlines, which had been leasing aircraft and crews from Norse Atlantic.
PIA was forced to cancel services from London to Islamabad and from Manchester to Lahore last night.
In a statement on social media, PIA said all UK flights from today would be operated by aircraft from its own fleet.
Londoner Fred was due to fly to Cape Town with Norse Atlantic next week for a friend's wedding, but has decided to change his plans.
"Yesterday the exact same flight I was going to get was cancelled, so I was worried" he told the BBC's Your Voice. "I sent an email to Norse and have had nothing back".
He decided to rebook with a different airline, which cost him an extra £1,000.
Another traveller, Claire from Herefordshire, is currently on holiday in South Africa with her daughter.
"We are due to fly home on Sunday and are now looking at a really expensive flight back," she said.
"We have tried contacting Norse but have been given an automated response saying someone will get back to us within three business days."
Advice for passengers
Consumer group Which? advised Norse Atlantic customers to not cancel their flights as the airline has not collapsed. Doing so removes passengers' right to a refund.
Rory Boland, Which? travel editor, said: "If Norse does go bust, whether you get a refund depends on how you booked and paid. If you booked directly with Norse, you may be able to claim a refund via your credit card provider, or possibly your debit card."
Those stuck abroad should contact insurers, while those with package holidays reliant on Norse Atlantic flights should contact their tour operator, he added.
Low-cost, long-haul
Norse Atlantic Airways was set up in 2021 as a low-cost long-haul carrier.
It entered the market as a successor to Norwegian, which withdrew from long haul routes as a result of the Covid pandemic.
But it has struggled to make money, and its problems worsened this year, as fuel prices rocketed due to the war in Iran.
Unlike many other European carriers, Norse Atlantic does not hedge its fuel bills by buying supplies in advance at fixed prices.
This left it fully exposed when prices soared, with the cost of fuel roughly doubling at one point. Prices remain significantly higher than they were before the conflict began.
In the first half of the year Norse Atlantic made a loss of $94.6m.
In a statement to the Oslo Stock Exchange on Wednesday, the airline said its financial situation was critical, and it needed to find short term funding to continue its operations.
After suspending flights, the company said it was in discussions with investors regarding a possible takeover that "would include a financing solution".
However, it said if short term funding could not be found quickly, "it is the Board's intention to file for bankruptcy".
Norse Atlantic had been looking for a buyer since July.
Its potential collapse follows the failure of Spirit Airlines in May. The US-based low cost carrier was another airline left exposed when jet fuel prices soared, as US airlines typically do not hedge their purchases.
If Norse Atlantic were to fold, it would be another major setback for the low-cost long-haul model, which has typically proved popular with passengers but difficult to sustain financially, especially on transatlantic routes.
Additional reporting by Rozina Sini

Laptop prices have been a hot-button topic all year long thanks to RAMageddon. We’ve seen existing models get more expensive and new models arrive at higher prices than last-gen — sometimes with lower specs, too. 2026 in general has been a bad time to buy a computer. But thankfully, Black Friday can offer a tiny bit of respite.
Buying a laptop right now is rough, but these Black Friday deals help
Despite the year’s bevy of price increases, this is the best time to buy a new laptop (if you absolutely need to).
If the computer you currently use is working well for you, good. Happy for you. But if you’re in the market for something more powerful, bigger, smaller, thinner, etc. — and you prefer new over used — you can currently save some decent dough on MacBooks, Windows notebooks, and gaming laptops.
The big, multi-hundred-dollar savings are typically reserved for higher-end models with bigger specs, like Windows models running Nvidia’s new Arm-based RTX Spark platform — which are getting their first major discounts. More budget-friendly laptops are also receiving decent discounts right now, though, including the MacBook Neo. The current $100 discount on Apple’s budget laptop might seem like small potatoes considering it’s just a return to its original price, but it’s the best you can do for now (if you can’t hold out for a rumored new one in 2027).
As always, the deals below are for laptops we’ve reviewed or tested firsthand in some way at The Verge — either that exact model or a similar one we expect to be equally good. There are many laptops out there and myriad deals running now through Cyber Monday, but if any of these fit your needs, you can’t go wrong.

Apple’s latest MacBook Air features an M5 chip and starts with 512GB of storage, with significantly faster performance than prior generations. It’s still an excellent machine despite the higher base price, especially given the battery life and speakers. Read our review.

Apple gave its base model MacBook Pro an upgrade to a 12-core M6 chip for 2026, and not a whole lot else. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Pro (M5 Pro / Max, 2026)
The non-OLED M5 Pro and M5 Max versions of the MacBook Pro deliver exceptional power and speed, making them ideal for creative work while offering best-in-class battery life. Read our review.
Apple MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display
Apple’s latest (and priciest) MacBook Pro is its first with a touchscreen. The new PROLED models pack familiar M5 Pro and M5 Max chips into a thinner and lighter chassis. Read our review.
Dell XPS 13 (2026)
The best XPS 13 in years. It doesn’t have a headphone jack and starts with only 8GB of RAM but it’s an excellent thin-and-light with a great screen, a solid Intel chip, and speedy storage.

An incredibly nice feeling laptop with great battery life and a love-it-or-hate-it latticeless keyboard. Read our review.

The latest 13-inch Surface Laptop has lower starting specs than the 2025 model, with 8GB of RAM, but it’s still a well put together laptop that now feels better to use after some notable Windows 11 updates. Read our review.

One of Lenovo’s new alternatives to the MacBook Neo, with an Intel Wildcat Lake chip, 8GB of RAM, and plenty of ports.

Asus Zenbook A14
Asus’s Zenbook A14 is a bit of a MacBook Air wannabe, but it’s a compelling alternative if you value a superlight Windows laptop with long battery life. The 2025 model sports an Arm-based Qualcomm Snapdragon X Plus chip, a nice 14-inch OLED display, and a sleek textured beige finish.
Asus Zenbook Duo (2026)
An expensive but excellent dual-screen laptop with a powerful Intel Panther Lake chip. The only thing better than a lovely OLED display is having two of them. Read our review.
HP OmniBook 3 16 (Intel Core 7)
An affordable laptop with a large 16-inch display, plenty of ports, and a solid Intel Core 7 350 chip. Read our review of the Snapdragon model.

The Omen 15 isn’t as affordable as its previous generations, but its upgrade to an RTX 50-series GPU means access to Nvidia’s latest DLSS game enhancements. Read our review.
Asus ROG Zephyrus G14 (2026, Intel)
Our favorite do-it-all gaming laptop for everyday use and portability got a nice upgrade for 2026: a faster Intel chip, better battery life, and a full-size SD card slot. It also got much more expensive than its previous generation. Read our review.
HP OmniBook 5 14 (2025, Snapdragon X Plus)
A nice OLED, 16GB of RAM, and marathon battery life in a travel-friendly laptop with an affordalbe price. Build quality is only okay. Read our review.
Acer Swift Air 14
Acer’s answer to the MacBook Neo, with an Intel Core 5 processor, 8GB of RAM, 120Hz display, and speedy Thunderbolt 4 ports. We’re still testing this one, but it’s showing promise for an affordable choice.
Acer Swift Go 16 AI
One of Acer’s most affordable 16-inch laptops can be had with either Intel or AMD chips. The large display isn’t particularly bright or high resolution but it’s got a smooth 120Hz refresh.
Lenovo Legion 5i 15.3-inch (2025)
Impressive specs with a step-up RTX 5060 GPU and sleek white chassis color for a low-$1,000 price when discounted.
Lenovo Yoga 7i 2-in-1
A good Intel Panther Lake chip, lots of RAM, and an OLED display combined in a 2-in-1 chassis.
Asus ProArt P14 (RTX Spark)
The entry-point into RTX Spark laptops, the Asus ProArt is a creator-focused MacBook Pro wannabe with a sleek blacked-out design and lovely OLED display. Read our initial Nvidia RTX Spark hands-on.

Lenovo’s Yoga Pro 9n with an 18-core RTX Spark chip has a high quality 15.3-inch OLED display and comes with a stylus you can use either on its screen or trackpad. Read our initial Nvidia RTX Spark hands-on.

Making a 3D fantasy map like a 16th-Century atlas
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:02:52 UTC
23:02:52 KST

Making a Fantasy Map Like a 16th-Century Atlas
Hugo -
Over the last few weeks, I worked on a map of various game dev techniques for people to refer to and learn from. Like, a real map! With each town being a technique's home, provided with a concise, illustrated example. And here we are. Today we are diving into the making of The Game Dev Atlas.
That's so 1570, bro.
If you want to pull off a cohesive art direction, you need to spend most of your time learning about your reference materials. No C code, no IDE, no sub-agents doing things. Just explore and wander on Google Images (old-school, I know), Wikipedia, and online museum galleries.
Gather things, ideas, and visuals! You should feel the inspiration (literally, as you inspire fresh air) inflating your mind, and the first scraps of decisions starting to flow through your head, like: I want a bit of that, none of that, this thing specifically for that part etc.
That is the whole work: what do you want? It's on you to set the quality bar and how things should be.
If you don't already know, typography conveys most of the design weight. That's just it.
Example: after some research I stumbled on that Luminari font that was a great fit for what I wanted my map to look like. It is a paid font, and there were plenty of free alternatives, but not quite as right as the Luminari font. Instead of saying "that's ok, this free one is the same vibe", I just paid for the font I found just right. Never concede terrain on your instinct!
A real island.
Claude, please generate a procedural terr...
Just pick an existing, real island. Nowadays we do have precise elevation data for most islands on Earth. If you want a realistic island, well, pick a real one.
Engraving in real time.
Some styles and colourings I've tried (click on the tabs to compare):
Don't be perfect!
Add some imperfections, color blends, etc. Nothing is perfect in real life, and having an eye for imperfections is what makes things look interesting.
Making towns interesting.
The point of the atlas is to showcase game dev techniques, one per town. So I decided two things:
First, I wanted small visual touches on each town's shape reflecting the technique it's about. For instance, the Procedural Legs town shows a crawling house.
Second, I wanted organic town buildings, so I built a small procedural building system to add diversity to buildings. Risky move: I'm a big fan of Houdini, so I could have thrown away the whole thing, and this blog post would be titled "I'm making Houdini, but running in the browser".
Anyway, here's what my small system can build:
60fps on the web.
The map application itself is written in C using the Kaigen engine as a headless C lib. I'm a Rust guy, but I must admit the compile time freaks me out for game dev with Rust. It took me several iterations to reach smooth 60fps in the browser, especially with the night mode (try it!).
But I'm finally happy with how it runs right now, while I'm still tweaking things atm.
The web app is written in Svelte, because I like Svelte. So the shallow routing and anything UI happen as part of the SvelteKit app.
Conclusion
It was a very fun project! At the end of the day, I love the creative process of making things, shaping ideas like a material, refining details. I strongly believe that when artists leverage AI, y'all will be cooked; because they alone know how to touch the human soul and create the surprise.
The Game Dev Atlas
Acknowledgments and tools used
- Sprite Fusion for the pixel art in the techniques
- Svelte and SvelteKit for the web app
- Luminari by Canada Type for the lettering
- EB Garamond Initials by Georg Duffner (SIL Open Font License) for the illuminated initials
- Kaigen for the engine
- NASA Earth Observatory for the photo of Kerguelen (28 October 2016)
- Terrain Tiles on AWS by Mapzen, and the data sources they name, for Kerguelen's elevation

‘The Book of the Human Body’ Surveys 2,000 Years of Remarkable Anatomical Inquiry
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
14:00:00 UTC
23:00:00 KST

‘The Book of the Human Body’ Surveys 2,000 Years of Remarkable Anatomical Inquiry
As long as humans have been around, we’ve been curious about existential questions like, Why are we here? And how do things work? That goes for our own bodies, too. But in our era of modern science and medicine, it’s difficult to imagine a time when physicians weren’t familiar with all of the bones in the skeleton, how a baby grows inside a womb, or how the cardiovascular system works. Even the profession that comes to mind when we think of a doctor didn’t really emerge until the past few centuries. During the medieval period, barber-surgeons were the primary medical practitioners (yes, the people who cut hair also conducted surgeries).
As medicine morphed into a scientific and intellectual pursuit, academies cropped up starting in the late 1700s, such as the College of Philadelphia—the first medical school in the U.S.—and the Edinburgh School of Surgery in Scotland. Ethics were, let’s say, malleable. In Edinburgh, a legal shortage of fresh cadavers used for study in the early 1800s spawned a shady underground trade in bodies, many of which were dug up from new burials in the dead of night. And then there were the famous serial murders committed by William Burke and William Hare, who cashed in by selling well over a dozen of their victims’ corpses to anatomist Robert Knox for use during his lectures. Suffice it to say, some scholars really wanted to understand our innards.
Slated for release in January, The Book of the Human Body delves into the art and science of anatomy, disease, reproduction, and more. Published by TASCHEN, the two-volume set compiles more than 950 images from over 75 rare books and manuscripts from around the world. From ancient analyses to medieval studies to modern diagrams, the anthology surveys the breadth of eras and cultural influences that have shaped our understanding of our physical selves.
Authored by medical historian Richard Barnett, the book celebrates universality and unquenched curiosity. Despite sleeping, breathing, and walking around in our bodies every day, we are often still mystified by much of what makes them work. Throughout history, that sense of wonder has remained constant. Barnett says, “Whatever they might represent, whatever they might express, these extraordinary images have lost none of their power to shock or captivate, to teach or inspire.”
Illustrations include a silk manuscript dated to 168 B.C.E., discovered in a tomb in southern China, which features 44 figures engaged in exercises of “self-cultivation,” plus excerpts from Andreas Vesalius’s anatomical atlas, Fabrica, published in 1543. “In all of these examples, the illustrations stand out not only as objects of medical understanding, but as conspicuous works of art in their own right,” the publisher says.
Pre-order your copy on Bookshop.

Jason Arday's research cleared in investigation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:50:05 UTC
22:50:05 KST

Jason Arday research for Queen's University cleared in investigation
Research by academic Jason Arday, who was accused of plagiarism before his death, has been cleared by one of the institutions that was investigating him.
Queen's University Belfast (QUB) began looking into Jason Arday's work after he resigned from his University of Cambridge roles.
The university had published a paper on its academic research site co-authored by Arday and other academics from Queen's.
In a statement, a spokesperson for QUB said: "Having reviewed the matter, we are satisfied that the paper meets the appropriate academic standards and have no concerns regarding its publication."
The paper, published in 2021, was corrected in 2024 to clarify that it used methodologies similar to a previous study Arday had written, which in turn had been based on another research paper published in 2016 by a team of academics at Brighton and Sussex Medical School.
Arday, who became Cambridge's youngest black professor in 2023, was found dead in Battersea on 14 August, having resigned as a professor of sociology of education the week prior.
The 41-year-old's death followed weeks of media coverage over plagiarism allegations and questions about his achievements. He denied the claims. An inquest into his death is under way.
Prime Minister Andy Burnham described the death of the former professor as "a tragedy on so many levels".
Accusations of plagiarism in Arday's work were first voiced by Nathan Cofnas - a US researcher and self-defined "race realist".
Cofnas has previously written that black people are less intelligent than other groups and would "disappear from almost all high-profile positions outside of sports and entertainment" under a meritocratic system.
He claimed on the online publishing platform Substack that he had put Arday's work through a plagiarism detection software, which found that "many passages are lifted with minimal editing, sometimes retaining copy-editing mistakes from the original source".
Following Arday's death, Cofnas was suspended from his post at Ghent University, after it launched an investigation into him "for discriminating against" the former Cambridge professor. He was later handed a three-month teaching ban.
Public figures and friends of Arday had called for a public inquiry into media coverage surrounding the former professor before his death.
Labour MP Diane Abbott and actress Jameela Jamil were among 30,000 signatories of a letter to the prime minister, which claims Arday's death followed "two weeks of relentless harassment".
The University of Cambridge launched a two-part independent review examining the support available to Arday before his death, as well as the wider context of his time at the university.
Liverpool John Moores University - which awarded Arday his PhD in 2015 - concluded that he had not plagiarised work.
Other institutions including Glasgow University, Durham University and Queen's University Belfast all began investigating Arday's work or his appointments to various positions.
Back in August, Queen's University said it was "looking into" a paper, published in 2021, on the impact of what was taught in universities on "Black, Asian and Minority Ethnic (BAME) learners" and "academics of colour".
The paper was called "Attempting to break the chain: Reimaging inclusive pedagogy and decolonising the curriculum within the academy".

You can grab a 12-pack of Zepath’s rechargeable AA batteries for just $24
Each set of AAs includes a clamshell charger, which lets you top off multiple batteries with a single connection.
My colleague Sean Hollister tested Zepath’s higher-capacity 3,600mWh batteries against competing lithium-ion AAs in April, and he picked them as his top choice. In his tests, they even outlasted Panasonic’s pricier Eneloop Pros in both a Game Boy Advance and a high-powered flashlight. Zepath also says the batteries deliver a steady 1.5V until they’re nearly empty and hold their charge well when not in use.
The best part about Zepath’s rechargeables, though, is how you charge them. Instead of relying on a multi-headed USB-C cable that plugs into each cell, you simply drop them into the included clamshell charging case, like a pair of AirPods or a similar set of wireless earbuds. The case also charges via USB-C, so you can top off eight batteries at once with a single connection in about two and a half hours.

She Reported Her Sexual Assault. The Marine Corps Kicked Her Out
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:06:34 UTC
22:06:34 KST

Marina was training at the Basic School in Quantico, Virginia, when she attended the Marine Corps Ball in November 2023. The annual celebration of the Corps’ birthday was supposed to be a night of revelry and camaraderie. Instead, Marina said, a lieutenant sexually assaulted her at knifepoint.
“It should have been the safest place to be,” she said, “and it was quite literally the most dangerous.”
At Marina’s request, The War Horse is using a pseudonym to protect her identity. Her identity was verified and parts of her account were corroborated through interviews and documents, including a military protective order against her alleged assailant, her retirement and discharge paperwork, and emails from the Marine Corps Special Trial Counsel. The War Horse does not identify survivors of sexual assault without their explicit consent.
Marina initially hesitated to report the assault.
“I had not heard great things in the past [about] the way people were treated after reporting this kind of thing,” she said.
Marina would later say that what her command put her through was worse than the assault itself.
Within about a week, she decided to tell military authorities about the attack. First she filed a “restricted” report, which would allow her to receive support and medical care without automatically triggering an investigation, but ultimately changed her report to an “unrestricted” report, which launched an investigation and notified the leaders of her unit.
“I did it more for my own peace of mind than anything else,” she said, “because I genuinely don’t think I could have slept at night knowing what I did, and then just letting him walk around.”
Marina struggled in the months afterward. Mental health challenges prevented her from continuing in training, and she eventually sought treatment for post-traumatic stress disorder.
“I hadn’t been doing great,” she said, “but I was working really hard to get better.”
She thought the Naval Criminal Investigative Service would thoroughly investigate the matter, and the Office of Special Trial Counsel, which launched about seven weeks after her assault to investigate violent offenses, would prosecute her assailant.
Instead, five months later, Marina received a shocking letter: She was being kicked out of the military—not the lieutenant who had allegedly sexually assaulted her. It would be an administrative separation, which the military uses to involuntarily separate service members with a lower burden of proof than a court-martial.
Marina’s paperwork cited a military doctor’s recommendation that she should be discharged after dealing with mental health issues from her assault. The official diagnosis, and the reason for the discharge, was “adjustment disorder with depressed mood.” Her commander recommended that she receive a general instead of an honorable discharge, which would strip her of veterans’ education benefits.
Her name was also entered into the Marine Officer Disciplinary Notebook, also known as the “Black Book,” a database the Marine Corps uses to track officer misconduct.
“Whatever happened initially from that assault has just been insurmountably added to by the way my command treated me,” Marina said. “If you’re put under an administrative discharge, everybody just automatically assumes that you’re a criminal.”
Marina hired a lawyer to fight the discharge and to have her name removed from the Black Book. It took years of appeals, but she was finally able to leave the Marines in the spring of 2026 with a medical retirement for post-traumatic stress disorder, instead of the administrative discharge.
It was a bitter victory for Marina, who had only about a week to pack up her apartment on base and had to stay with her parents for a couple of weeks before finding an apartment.
Marina’s story isn’t unique.
In 2021, the Pentagon launched a coordinated effort to combat sexual assault in the military. But even after organizational changes, service members are still being discharged not long after reporting their assaults, an investigation from The War Horse found.
Over the past five years, 30 Marines—12 women and 18 men—have been kicked out of the military through the administrative discharge process within two years of reporting their sexual assault, according to public records obtained by The War Horse. Most were discharged for “misconduct,” classified as either drug or alcohol abuse or another serious offense, though some were separated for “unsatisfactory performance” or medical conditions that did not rise to the level of disability.
This process is often overseen by the survivor’s chain of command. Advocates for military sexual assault victims say the practice is military-wide, but is especially common in the Marines, the most male-dominated military branch.
One of the survivors who spoke with The War Horse said she was told less than a year after reporting her sexual assault that she would be administratively separated, a potential red flag for retaliation highlighted in Department of Defense regulations that can warrant review from a senior officer. Another was told of her separation 13 months after her assault, just outside that window.
“We strictly enforce statutory and regulatory protections to ensure all Marines can report sexual misconduct, free from fear of retaliation,” a Marine Corps spokesperson said when asked for comment on Marines who have been administratively separated after reporting sexual assault.
“Victims of sexual assault are provided with an independent Victim’s Legal Counsel to advocate for and defend a victim’s rights, including protection from any retaliation. Claims of retaliation are referred to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service or the [Department of Defense] Office of Inspector General for independent investigation,” the spokesperson said.
“I have no doubt that every woman who will report a sexual assault understands that it could cost them their career,” said Rita Graham, policy director at Service Women’s Action Network (SWAN), an advocacy group for women in the military. “Survivors should not have to choose between reporting a crime and staying in uniform.”
Service members and members of the military community affected by sexual assault can contact the DoD Safe Helpline for confidential support 24/7 at 877-995-5247 or through online chat at SafeHelpline.org. Anyone affected by sexual assault can also contact RAINN’s National Sexual Assault Hotline at 800-656-HOPE (4673), text “HOPE” to 64673, or chat at RAINN.org.
An ‘Inconceivable’ Amount of Power
Administrative separations have long been a tool used by the military to get rid of those it perceives as causing trouble, according to victim advocates and researchers.
In contrast to a court-martial, the military’s version of a legal trial, there are no rules of evidence or procedural law during an administrative separation, said Francesca Graham, vice president of Walk the Talk Foundation, a nonprofit that defends military whistleblowers.
Most courts-martial are overseen by a judge and decided by a panel—similar to a jury in a civilian criminal trial—but administrative separations often allow for a service member’s immediate chain of command to decide their fate.
“The amount of power that is inherent in this process, it’s almost inconceivable,” Graham said.
The use of courts-martial has declined sharply in recent decades, raising concerns that military commanders have increasingly relied on administrative separations to address misconduct. In 2018, Defense Secretary Jim Mattis warned commanders against taking the easier route over pursuing military justice proceedings.
“Administrative actions should not be the default method to address illicit conduct simply because it is less burdensome than the military justice system,” Mattis wrote.
The U.S. military’s treatment of sexual assault survivors has also faced international scrutiny. In 2015, advocates filed petitions with the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, alleging that the U.S. military failed to adequately address sexual violence and retaliated against service members who reported it. Similar concerns have been raised before the United Nations.
“We have clients who’ve said, ‘The trauma of the sexual assault that I experienced, it was horrific,’” said Elizabeth Brundige, a law professor at Cornell University’s Gender Justice Clinic who represents over two dozen military sexual assault victims. “But it was really the lack of response and retaliation by my superiors that was the real torture or the real violation that I felt.”
Have you been retaliated against for reporting a sexual assault in the military?
The War Horse is continuing to report on military sexual assault and retaliation. If you have firsthand knowledge, documents, internal communications, or other information that could help our reporting, email us at tips@thewarhorse.org or learn how to submit a tip securely here. We take source protection seriously and will not publish your name without your permission.
Service members with at least six years of military service and those at risk of an other-than-honorable discharge can request to appear before an administrative separation board of three other service members—usually two officers and a senior enlisted member—to make their case. Otherwise, the decision to retain them rests solely with their chain of command.
“You’re just really relying on a commander to make the right call, and sometimes they do, sometimes they don’t, and that’s when it gets really scary,” said Rita Graham from SWAN.
Administrative discharge can have long-term consequences. Though some are characterized as honorable and general discharges, those discharged with other-than-honorable discharges—known colloquially as “bad paper”—can face lifelong challenges.
“It marks you for life,” said Stephanie Schroeder, a Marine veteran who reported being sexually assaulted in 2002 and was later discharged from the service with a personality disorder, despite never being seen by a therapist to diagnose the condition. “Any type of federal job you want, any type of background check, it shows up.”
It’s difficult to determine exactly how many have been impacted by “bad paper” discharges, but a 2025 report from the Government Accountability Office found that between January 2018 and March 2024, more than 3,000 service members petitioned for discharge upgrades with cases that involved sexual harassment or assault. A 2016 Human Rights Watch report also found that potentially thousands of service members had been administratively discharged between 1960 and 2015 with bad paper in retaliation for reporting sexual assault.
“If people are still being punished for reporting a sexual assault, then that is a huge problem,” said Sara Darehshori, who authored the Human Rights Watch report.
In 2025, about 6.4% of active-duty Marine Corps women said they had been sexually assaulted in the prior 12 months, according to the Pentagon’s report on sexual assault in the military—more than any other branch. The number of men in the Marine Corps who experienced a sexual assault was 1%, on par with other branches.
“They say the Marines are top-notch when they’re doing something. Whether it’s good or bad, they’re gonna do it 150%,” said Amy Franck, a former Army sexual assault program manager and founder of Never Alone, a nonprofit organization that provides resources to survivors of military sexual assault. “I have never seen such strategic, in-sync retaliation.”
According to Franck, those 30 Marines who were administratively discharged over the past five years don’t show the full picture. The figure doesn’t take into account people whom the Marines tried and failed to administratively separate, or those who, like Marina, used a lawyer to change the administrative discharge to another type of discharge, like a medical discharge.
When it comes to Marines who have been sexually assaulted and then threatened with an administrative discharge, “I can count 10 myself,” Franck said.
‘I Still Mourn the Life That I Wanted’
In the fall of 2021, Rosemary, a 24-year-old Marine stationed at Camp Pendleton, went to a local bar with a male colleague and his friends. Afterward, she offered to let him stay on a blow-up mattress in her apartment, which was closer to downtown, instead of driving back to his place after a night of drinking.
When she woke up the next morning, her body felt sore and she was surprised to find her underwear on backward.
That day, a friend took Rosemary to a military medical center for a forensic sexual assault exam. Rosemary brought along her underwear and a towel left on the floor. Then she reported the assault to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service. The agency opened an investigation and conducted a digital forensics download of her phone.
In March 2022, the NCIS investigation determined that “no further investigative action was deemed necessary.” But like Marina, several months later Rosemary learned that she was under investigation herself.
Rosemary is married to another service member, who had been training on a different base on the night of the alleged assault. Now, she was being accused of lying about her sexual assault and committing adultery, which is a crime in the military.
“This can’t be real,” she remembered thinking. “You’re kidding.”
The War Horse interviewed Rosemary multiple times, corroborated parts of her story with a friend, and reviewed dozens of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation, an inspector general complaint, her notification of administrative separation, and excerpts from her journal.
The War Horse also reviewed Rosemary’s military fitness reports and showed them to a retired Marine colonel. He said the evaluations showed that Rosemary had been in good standing before the alleged assault, with her commanding officer continuously recommending her for promotion.
Yet in March 2023, Rosemary’s commander recommended her for administrative separation over the allegations of adultery and lying about her sexual assault. The paperwork cited “substandard performance of duty, misconduct, and moral or professional dereliction.”
Her case went before an administrative discharge board in the spring of 2024. At the hearing, Rosemary explained the assault to three strangers who would decide her fate in the military.
“I couldn’t run from the truth anymore. He chose me. … He raped me. And I was going to implode,” she wrote in her journal the day before the hearing.
Amy Franck, Rosemary’s victim advocate, was in the room with her that day, incensed.
“Her husband doesn’t think that she was cheating,” Franck told The War Horse. “So how the fuck does the Marine Corps think she was cheating?”
The board voted 2-1 to allow Rosemary to stay in the Marines.
By that point, however, she had already made her choice.
“I was done,” she said.
She voluntarily left the Marines six months later when her contract ended with an honorable discharge.
Rosemary had wanted to be in the Marine Corps since she was 8 years old. She had worked out with the Marine Corps delayed-entry program while in high school, then received an ROTC scholarship for college. She intended to stay in until retirement, but that plan changed.
“I loved it,” she said. “I still mourn the life that I wanted.”
No More ‘Walking on Eggshells’
Last year, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth ordered a review of the military’s equal opportunity programs, directing officials to dismiss complaints lacking credible evidence and protect service members from knowingly false allegations.
The changes were part of a broader effort Hegseth described as ending a military culture of “walking on eggshells.”
In October 2026, Hegseth announced further changes to the military’s inspector general complaint process, which handles allegations of misconduct and abuses of authority.
“Commanders are now back in charge,” he said, describing the inspector general process as having been “weaponized.” He said investigations would no longer be launched based on “speculation, personal gripes, or hearsay.”
But Pentagon data show that false sexual assault claims are exceptionally rare. In 2025, the military found that only about 1% of cases were “unfounded,” meaning investigators determined the allegations were false or baseless.
Hegseth’s policy changes marked a major shift. For several years, the military was attempting to reform how it handled sexual assault and harassment complaints. Those changes followed years of pressure from survivors and advocates, intensified by the 2020 murder of Army Spc. Vanessa Guillén at Fort Hood, Texas. An Army investigation later found that Guillén had reported sexual harassment before her death but supervisors failed to elevate the complaints.
In 2021, at President Joe Biden’s direction, Defense Secretary Lloyd Austin ordered an independent review of sexual assault in the military. Its nearly 300-page report made more than 80 recommendations, including moving the responsibility for prosecuting serious crimes, like sexual assaults, away from commanders.
Lindsey Knapp, an Army veteran, attorney, and advocate for military sexual assault survivors, said Hegseth’s changes would discourage service members from coming forward.
“That creates a chilling effect for people already trying to report in an environment where reporting sexual assault was already horrendous,” Knapp said.
But several military lawyers interviewed by The War Horse disagreed, saying the pendulum had swung too far in favor of sexual assault survivors.
“A lot of times I do feel as though people’s careers are ruined over accusations that, in my opinion, are not supported by the preponderance of the evidence,” said Matthew Barry, a former Army judge advocate.
Gary Barthel, a retired Marine judge advocate, said he has never seen negative action taken against an alleged sexual assault victim just because of their report.
Typically, he said, survivors face charges when they also engage in misconduct, like underage drinking. In that case, Barthel said it was fair to discipline the survivor.
“I think they have to be held accountable if there’s some misconduct,” he said. “Because really, the misconduct that’s associated with it kind of put them in a position that caused them to become a victim.” (That view conflicts with the military’s Safe-to-Report policy, which since 2022 has barred commanders from disciplining sexual assault survivors for minor collateral misconduct such as underage drinking.)
While reports of sexual assault in the military have decreased between 2023 and 2025, the small amount of public data that exists shows that retaliation remains a military-wide problem.
In its annual report on sexual assault, the Pentagon found that more than 50 service members, most of whom were junior enlisted and women, said they were retaliated against after reporting their assault. They typically said the person retaliating against them was “a superior in the chain of command.”
Only 16 of those complaints were investigated. None resulted in disciplinary action against the alleged attacker.
“There is a long, pervasive tradition of this happening,” said Josh Connolly, senior vice president of Protect Our Defenders, a nonprofit group that works to end sexual misconduct in the military, “and it is still occurring.”
12 Pullups
Elizabeth’s appointment with a Navy doctor in the fall of 2023 seemed routine. The doctor, a lieutenant commander, and a Navy corpsman listened as she described feeling nerve pain after a car crash.
Then the doctor asked the corpsman to leave. After the door closed, Elizabeth said, he began touching her inappropriately over her shorts.
Elizabeth quickly reported the assault, and the next day NCIS opened an investigation that lasted seven months. The doctor refused to be interviewed, invoking his right to remain silent. NCIS ultimately closed the case, citing “insufficient evidence for probable cause.”
In the months afterward, Elizabeth said instructors at the Basic School refused to coach her. She was also ordered not to enter the building where her sexual assault victim advocate worked.
“I reported it because I thought it was the right thing to do,” Elizabeth said. “I ended up regretting it.”
Then, in August 2024, Elizabeth took a physical fitness test. She reported completing 12 pullups—as she had several times in the past, according to military records—but the sergeant overseeing the pullup test later claimed she had only done five, fewer than she had ever done.
Two weeks later, Elizabeth was pulled aside during a field exercise and told she was under investigation for allegedly falsifying her physical fitness test score.
Elizabeth had no reason to lie. Even if she had completed only five pullups, that still would have been enough to pass the test. She said her commander had allowed everyone else in her unit to retake the test but denied her the same opportunity.
Soon after, Elizabeth received a disciplinary counseling. About six weeks later, her commanding officer, a colonel, recommended her for administrative separation.
The War Horse interviewed Elizabeth multiple times, and reviewed hundreds of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation into her alleged assault, a command-directed investigation into the fitness test allegations, her administrative separation notification, and 16 letters from current and former service members recommending that she be retained.
Elizabeth fought the administrative separation, filing multiple appeals. But because she had served fewer than six years, she was not entitled to present her case before an administrative separation board.
“All they had to do was let her take the physical fitness test over,” said her lawyer, Ruth Cresenzo, a former Army National Guard judge advocate and executive director of CHANCE, a nonprofit organization that provides legal advocacy to crime victims.
Cresenzo said the ordeal was “straight up retaliation” and “the craziest case I’ve ever seen.”
In September 2026, Elizabeth learned she would not be granted an administrative separation board hearing. Instead, she was honorably discharged from the Marines in October. But her discharge paperwork, known as a DD-214, cites misconduct for a “serious offense” as the reason for her separation.
Now out of the Marine Corps, Elizabeth is trying to find a new job and move out of her apartment. She doesn’t know where she’ll end up.
Still, she hasn’t given up on being a Marine. She plans to petition the Board of Corrections of Naval Records, hoping to be reinstated.
“People have asked me what I want to do on the outside, and I don’t have an answer,” Elizabeth said. “This is what I want to do.”
This article from The War Horse was edited by Paul Szoldra, fact-checked by Jess Rohan, and copy-edited by Mitchell Hansen-Dewar.

She Reported Her Sexual Assault. The Marine Corps Kicked Her Out
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
13:06:34 UTC
22:06:34 KST

Marina was training at the Basic School in Quantico, Virginia, when she attended the Marine Corps Ball in November 2023. The annual celebration of the Corps’ birthday was supposed to be a night of revelry and camaraderie. Instead, Marina said, a lieutenant sexually assaulted her at knifepoint.
“It should have been the safest place to be,” she said, “and it was quite literally the most dangerous.”
At Marina’s request, The War Horse is using a pseudonym to protect her identity. Her identity was verified and parts of her account were corroborated through interviews and documents, including a military protective order against her alleged assailant, her retirement and discharge paperwork, and emails from the Marine Corps Special Trial Counsel. The War Horse does not identify survivors of sexual assault without their explicit consent.
Marina initially hesitated to report the assault.
“I had not heard great things in the past [about] the way people were treated after reporting this kind of thing,” she said.
Marina would later say that what her command put her through was worse than the assault itself.
Within about a week, she decided to tell military authorities about the attack. First she filed a “restricted” report, which would allow her to receive support and medical care without automatically triggering an investigation, but ultimately changed her report to an “unrestricted” report, which launched an investigation and notified the leaders of her unit.
“I did it more for my own peace of mind than anything else,” she said, “because I genuinely don’t think I could have slept at night knowing what I did, and then just letting him walk around.”
Marina struggled in the months afterward. Mental health challenges prevented her from continuing in training, and she eventually sought treatment for post-traumatic stress disorder.
“I hadn’t been doing great,” she said, “but I was working really hard to get better.”
She thought the Naval Criminal Investigative Service would thoroughly investigate the matter, and the Office of Special Trial Counsel, which launched about seven weeks after her assault to investigate violent offenses, would prosecute her assailant.
Instead, five months later, Marina received a shocking letter: She was being kicked out of the military—not the lieutenant who had allegedly sexually assaulted her. It would be an administrative separation, which the military uses to involuntarily separate service members with a lower burden of proof than a court-martial.
Marina’s paperwork cited a military doctor’s recommendation that she should be discharged after dealing with mental health issues from her assault. The official diagnosis, and the reason for the discharge, was “adjustment disorder with depressed mood.” Her commander recommended that she receive a general instead of an honorable discharge, which would strip her of veterans’ education benefits.
Her name was also entered into the Marine Officer Disciplinary Notebook, also known as the “Black Book,” a database the Marine Corps uses to track officer misconduct.
“Whatever happened initially from that assault has just been insurmountably added to by the way my command treated me,” Marina said. “If you’re put under an administrative discharge, everybody just automatically assumes that you’re a criminal.”
Marina hired a lawyer to fight the discharge and to have her name removed from the Black Book. It took years of appeals, but she was finally able to leave the Marines in the spring of 2026 with a medical retirement for post-traumatic stress disorder, instead of the administrative discharge.
It was a bitter victory for Marina, who had only about a week to pack up her apartment on base and had to stay with her parents for a couple of weeks before finding an apartment.
Marina’s story isn’t unique.
In 2021, the Pentagon launched a coordinated effort to combat sexual assault in the military. But even after organizational changes, service members are still being discharged not long after reporting their assaults, an investigation from The War Horse found.
Over the past five years, 30 Marines—12 women and 18 men—have been kicked out of the military through the administrative discharge process within two years of reporting their sexual assault, according to public records obtained by The War Horse. Most were discharged for “misconduct,” classified as either drug or alcohol abuse or another serious offense, though some were separated for “unsatisfactory performance” or medical conditions that did not rise to the level of disability.
This process is often overseen by the survivor’s chain of command. Advocates for military sexual assault victims say the practice is military-wide, but is especially common in the Marines, the most male-dominated military branch.
One of the survivors who spoke with The War Horse said she was told less than a year after reporting her sexual assault that she would be administratively separated, a potential red flag for retaliation highlighted in Department of Defense regulations that can warrant review from a senior officer. Another was told of her separation 13 months after her assault, just outside that window.
“We strictly enforce statutory and regulatory protections to ensure all Marines can report sexual misconduct, free from fear of retaliation,” a Marine Corps spokesperson said when asked for comment on Marines who have been administratively separated after reporting sexual assault.
“Victims of sexual assault are provided with an independent Victim’s Legal Counsel to advocate for and defend a victim’s rights, including protection from any retaliation. Claims of retaliation are referred to the Naval Criminal Investigative Service or the [Department of Defense] Office of Inspector General for independent investigation,” the spokesperson said.
“I have no doubt that every woman who will report a sexual assault understands that it could cost them their career,” said Rita Graham, policy director at Service Women’s Action Network (SWAN), an advocacy group for women in the military. “Survivors should not have to choose between reporting a crime and staying in uniform.”
Service members and members of the military community affected by sexual assault can contact the DoD Safe Helpline for confidential support 24/7 at 877-995-5247 or through online chat at SafeHelpline.org. Anyone affected by sexual assault can also contact RAINN’s National Sexual Assault Hotline at 800-656-HOPE (4673), text “HOPE” to 64673, or chat at RAINN.org.

Elizabeth’s appointment with a Navy doctor in the fall of 2023 seemed routine. The doctor, a lieutenant commander, and a Navy corpsman listened as she described feeling nerve pain after a car crash.
Then the doctor asked the corpsman to leave. After the door closed, Elizabeth said, he began touching her inappropriately over her shorts.
Elizabeth quickly reported the assault, and the next day NCIS opened an investigation that lasted seven months. The doctor refused to be interviewed, invoking his right to remain silent. NCIS ultimately closed the case, citing “insufficient evidence for probable cause.”
In the months afterward, Elizabeth said instructors at the Basic School refused to coach her. She was also ordered not to enter the building where her sexual assault victim advocate worked.
“I reported it because I thought it was the right thing to do,” Elizabeth said. “I ended up regretting it.”
Then, in August 2024, Elizabeth took a physical fitness test. She reported completing 12 pullups—as she had several times in the past, according to military records—but the sergeant overseeing the pullup test later claimed she had only done five, fewer than she had ever done.
Two weeks later, Elizabeth was pulled aside during a field exercise and told she was under investigation for allegedly falsifying her physical fitness test score.
Elizabeth had no reason to lie. Even if she had completed only five pullups, that still would have been enough to pass the test. She said her commander had allowed everyone else in her unit to retake the test but denied her the same opportunity.
Soon after, Elizabeth received a disciplinary counseling. About six weeks later, her commanding officer, a colonel, recommended her for administrative separation.
The War Horse interviewed Elizabeth multiple times, and reviewed hundreds of pages of records, including the NCIS investigation into her alleged assault, a command-directed investigation into the fitness test allegations, her administrative separation notification, and 16 letters from current and former service members recommending that she be retained.
Elizabeth fought the administrative separation, filing multiple appeals. But because she had served fewer than six years, she was not entitled to present her case before an administrative separation board.
“All they had to do was let her take the physical fitness test over,” said her lawyer, Ruth Cresenzo, a former Army National Guard judge advocate and executive director of CHANCE, a nonprofit organization that provides legal advocacy to crime victims.
Cresenzo said the ordeal was “straight up retaliation” and “the craziest case I’ve ever seen.”
In September 2026, Elizabeth learned she would not be granted an administrative separation board hearing. Instead, she was honorably discharged from the Marines in October. But her discharge paperwork, known as a DD-214, cites misconduct for a “serious offense” as the reason for her separation.
Now out of the Marine Corps, Elizabeth is trying to find a new job and move out of her apartment. She doesn’t know where she’ll end up.
Still, she hasn’t given up on being a Marine. She plans to petition the Board of Corrections of Naval Records, hoping to be reinstated.
“People have asked me what I want to do on the outside, and I don’t have an answer,” Elizabeth said. “This is what I want to do.”
This article from The War Horse was edited by Paul Szoldra, fact-checked by Jess Rohan, and copy-edited by Mitchell Hansen-Dewar.
