New York and California Age Gating Rules Go into Effect in the New Year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
12:43:40 UTC
21:43:40 KST

Come January 2027, everyday social media browsing in two of the country's largest states will look noticeably different. In California, AB 1709 (2026), AB 1043 (2025), and SB 976 (2024) are slated to take effect on January 1, 2027. Meanwhile, state attorney general regulations for New York’s Stop Addictive Feeds Exploitation (SAFE) for Kids Act take effect on January 25, 2027.
EFF has opposed these laws across legislatures, regulatory proceedings, and the courts. The core problem is simple: age-gating mandates trade away everyday user privacy and free expression under the guise of safety, while pushing platforms to gather even more personal data on users.
What California’s Rules Say
Three interlocking California laws take effect on January 1, 2027, reshaping how platforms handle age checks and design features for young users. First, SB 976 (2024) requires verifiable parental consent before platforms can offer algorithmic feeds or nighttime notifications to anyone under 18. Second, AB 1709 (2026) goes even further by outright banning "addictive features," including personalized recommendation algorithms, autoplay, and endless scroll, for users under 16, even with parental permission. Third, the Digital Age Assurance Act (AB 1043, 2025) mandates age-assurance checks across devices (operating systems) and apps, serving as the enforcement backbone for those restrictions. Together with the California Attorney General’s proposed rules on SB 976, which outline age-gating methods, accuracy, circumvention, and parental consent, these laws force platforms into a two-tier design that walls young people off from basic content-discovery tools, puts everyone's privacy at risk to prove their age, and has already drawn First Amendment lawsuits from social media companies against both SB 976 and AB 1709.
For example: rather than minimizing corporate surveillance and data collection by passing stronger privacy laws, the SB 976 regulations encourage companies to evaluate behavioral patterns, collected user data, digital interactions, and metadata to estimate age (Sec. 561(a)(2)), alongside location data collected for other purposes (Sec. 565). Good privacy laws should discourage collecting that information in the first place, not reward broader tracking.
At the same time, these age assurance rules build in steep error rates and weak safeguards. They acknowledge that age-verification tools can misclassify young people as adults, allowing false-positive rates of up to 8% for 16-year-olds and 15% for 17-year-olds (Sec. 561(c)(4)(iv)). Yet the rules set no benchmarks at all for false negatives, meaning adults (especially 18- to 20-year-olds) are likely to be wrongly flagged as under 18. They also recognize demographic bias in facial recognition, yet offer no concrete fix, vaguely telling companies to take mitigating measures like correcting dataset imbalances (Sec. 563(a)(6)). While users can appeal wrong age estimates, the rules set no firm deadlines, meaning mistakes can drag on without a prompt fix, blocking user access (Sec. 564). Worse, requiring platforms to let users report others they suspect are under the age of 18 (Sec. 567) invites targeted harassment by bad actors, while rules telling companies to account for users trying to “conceal or misrepresent” their California location (Sec. 565) risk penalizing everyday VPN users.
What New York’s Rules Say
Along the same lines as the California regulations, New York’s SAFE for Kids Act restricts personalized recommendations and nighttime notifications (between midnight and 6am) without required consent, and mandates that platforms run age assurance and determine user location. If a user does not verify their age or is estimated to be under-18, they will either be relegated to an account with no personalized recommendations and nighttime notifications (if the platform creates one), or prohibited from accessing the platform/service.
As we detailed in our December 2025 comments to the New York Attorney General, age-verification mandates inevitably chill access to online speech. And the final regulations introduce several troubling specifics.
The New York regulations use broad definitions for "age estimation," "age inference," and "age verification" (700.1(h)–(j)) that push companies to collect and retain more personal information from users. Even where privacy protections apply to that information, users cannot sue or enforce those rights themselves, since enforcement rests solely with the Attorney General (700.8). Worse, the rule’s definition of deletion appears to let companies "de-identify" age-assurance data and keep using it in that form, potentially feeding AI model training and creating re-identification risks if leaked or stolen. At the same time, platforms are required to hold onto records (including a user's age status and compliance data) for five years, increasing the risk of misuse or breaches (700.7(b)).
Echoing the problems in California, location and accuracy regulations bring further privacy and security risks. The regulations demand that operators must weigh all reliable location information and take reasonable steps to detect user efforts to “conceal or misrepresent” being in New York, which expands location tracking and threatens VPN use (700.2(d)(2)). On accuracy, the rules permit up to a 15% false-positive rate for 17-year-olds (700.1(b)), yet just like the California rules, say nothing about false negatives—meaning adults, especially 18- to 20-year-olds, are likely to be wrongly flagged as under 18. When that happens, the appeals process can take longer than 10 days, leaving misclassified adults locked out of standard features (or platforms altogether) for over a week.
What This Means for Users in NY and CA
Under both states' regimes, many users will be pressed to hand over personal information just to access standard features like personalized feeds or notifications, while social media companies are encouraged to monitor (and monetize) user behavior even more closely. Because these laws define "addictive feeds" so broadly that the label covers virtually every basic recommendation tool (including feeds built from who you follow, posts you like, or topics you say you are interested in), platforms face a stark choice: either build stripped-down versions of their apps without algorithmic feeds, likes, or notifications, or simply boot any account suspected of belonging to someone under 18. Either way, all users, and especially young people across both states, risk being shut out of major online spaces as they exist today.
When young people lose accounts or get pushed into stripped-down experiences, they lose contact with friends, support groups, community organizing, local news, creative audiences, and ways to speak out on current issues. U.S. law has long recognized that people under 18 have their own constitutional rights to speak and receive information, and sweeping age gates directly interfere with those rights.
At the same time, lawmakers often sell age-verification mandates as a way to rein in Big Tech, yet the opposite happens in practice. Megacorporations like Meta and Google have the resources to build expensive age-estimation systems and absorb steep fines (such as $50,000 per violation in California) while they figure out compliance. Smaller platforms and independent communities such as Bluesky or Dreamwidth, however, cannot afford those legal risks or engineering costs. As we have already seen in states like Mississippi, crushing compliance burdens push smaller sites out of the market and drive users back toward the biggest corporate giants. Instead of holding Big Tech accountable, these rules entrench its dominance; leaving everyone with fewer community options, less innovation, and a more centralized surveillance machine.
Help Us Help You
Legislative and regulatory action are setbacks, but they do not settle the debate. If these rules affect you in California, New York, or anywhere else: keep a record while the details are still fresh, and share it with our team at agegates@eff.org.
Take screenshots and note the date, time, service, and what you were trying to do; write down what you lost (whether that means an account, a club or volunteer group, contact with friends, local news, or a favorite creator) or note if you chose to walk away from a service rather than submit an ID or personal data. You do not need to hand over sensitive information to show that these rules burdened you. If you hit an age check or parental-consent snag, save what the platform asked for, whether it worked or failed, any privacy promises or laws cited, and any appeal attempts.
If these restrictions get in your way, email EFF at agegates@eff.org and tell us what happened. Tracking real-world lockouts, age-check failures, and infringements on free speech puts us in a stronger position to challenge the worst of these restrictions in legislatures and the courts. At the same time, we will keep fighting for laws that actually make people safer online by curbing corporate surveillance and giving parents, youth, and all internet users meaningful choice and control.

====================================================================================================

Kimi for Science More scientific ideas, made possible
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
12:03:11 UTC
21:03:11 KST

More resources for your research
Selected applicants will receive up to $20,000 in Kimi API credits (approx. 7B tokens), with the exact amount determined by research needs and review outcomes.
Science has always been driven by human curiosity, rigorous inquiry, and a passion for the unknown
A valuable hypothesis can require extensive literature review, data analysis, and repeated validation. An unexpected result can also spark the next breakthrough. Yet limited time, compute, and people mean many ideas worth exploring never receive the attention they deserve. We believe AI can accelerate research and expand the boundaries of discovery, helping researchers move beyond constraints of time, resources, and methodology and bringing more ambitious ideas within reach
From life sciences and health to energy and materials, and from fundamental science to the unknown, countless important questions remain unanswered. We hope Kimi can be a trusted partner for scientists, supporting researchers as they take on harder, more original problems
Give every bold idea the chance to be tested, and turn more discoveries of lasting value into reality
Kimi for Science welcomes researchers across the natural sciences, engineering, and related interdisciplinary fields to apply
We welcome research at every stage, from improving existing workflows with Kimi to exploring new methodologies and tackling more challenging scientific problems
Technical support from the Kimi team, plus early access to new models and curated research and community resources
Outstanding researchers and teams can access longer-term, more in-depth technical and resource support
You may submit your application more than once; only the most recent submission will be reviewed

Pizza history: AI agent's cardless purchase is a first for UK
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
11:41:11 UTC
20:41:11 KST

An order of Papa John’s margherita pizzas has become the first purchase in the UK to be made using an AI agent using cardless payment technology.
The order of nine mini pizzas was made as part of the Financial Conduct Authority’s (FCA) regulated sandbox trials to test the technology, using Lloyds Bank and payment specialist TrueLayer.
London-based TrueLayer, which enables payments to be made between bank accounts without using cards, said it was the first time an AI agent had been used to pay a UK retailer for an order for a real customer.
“We completed this payment transaction with Papa John’s with no account numbers, no password [and] handled with the highest possible security profile,” said the TrueLayer chief executive, Francesco Simoneschi, in an interview with BBC Radio 4’s Today programme. “We talked with the AI agent, described the occasion and dietary requirements. Of course the agent suggested different flavours and quantities. We confirmed the order and the pizza arrived later.”
The team ordered the pizzas using the Papa John’s AI agent, which had been given permission to access their bank account and request payment to the retailer’s account.
Papa John’s AI agent was set to work with a strict mandate established with Lloyds, including a maximum spend limit per transaction and per month, with no account security information shared.
Simoneschi said the test was the first step toward mass adoption by the public of a technology that would fundamentally change purchasing habits.
“This is possibly the last Black Friday as we know it,” he said, referring to the annual online sales discount day. “At the moment plenty of us are already using AI to write up the shopping list. I think next year the agent will buy gifts on our behalf. Agents will do shopping on our behalf, will manage our finances, and will help us understand and compare complex products. This idea that they can really help us to sort out a lot of different aspects of our lives is a little bit like having an assistant available to millions of consumers out there.”
However, Simoneschi said consumers would still need to make informed decisions about how they used AI agents for making purchases.
“I think good [personal] judgment still applies, of course,” he said. “It does require control and supervision, and we need to make sure that the underlying technology is fit for purpose, while enforcing the highest possible security standards.”
He added that if there was an error or mistake in the order process, such as the agent ordering the wrong product, consumers would still be protected by existing rules and regulations covering buyers’ rights.
Earlier this week the FCA issued a warning to financial services companies developing AI agents that they must comply with existing regulations.
“The reason we are testing with regulators in this sandbox is to really understand all the aspects of the technology ahead of its mass adoption,” he said. “This is a great place to start.”
Meta’s personal AI assistant, Muse, which launched in the UK on Tuesday, can also make purchases online. Unlike TrueLayer, Muse makes payments using a customer’s bank card.

James T. Russell, Physicist Who Pioneered CD and DVD Technology, Dies At 95
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

James T. Russell, Physicist Who Pioneered CD and DVD Technology, Dies At 95 (chronline.com) 13
			
		 	
				James T. Russell, the American physicist whose pioneering digital-to-optical recording and playback system laid the foundation for CD, DVD and Blu-ray technology, has died at the age of 95. "Russell never became a household name, but the technology he developed, later licensed by Sony and Phillips, would transform everything from the way people listen to music to the way data is stored," reports The Daily Chronicle. From the report:  In a career spanning almost his entire life, Russell earned more than 70 patents, but his biggest breakthrough was the invention of the first digital-to-optical recording and playback system in the 1960s and early 1970s, a system that brought crystal clear audio and film to the masses. [...] It was in Richland that Russell started exploring how to create cleaner recordings of music. Popping and hissing often accompanied the music of vinyl records, from the analog sound of a needle scraping against microgrooves, and Russell thought he could do something about it. He crunched the numbers before bringing an invention report to Battelle: What if a light beam could read a digital record?
"It isn't clear what the problem was," Russell recalled in a Seattle Times interview in 2004. "I think there was some feeling amongst the moneybags that it probably wouldn't work, (or) if it did work there's no advantage, no value in optical records. What would you use it for, and would people want it and, anyway, if it was any good, IBM would have already invented it." While his idea faced skepticism early on, the company gave him some money to experiment. Russell invented an early method to store audio digitally and play it back to produce a flawless sound. He also created a way to store digital information densely.
Other primitive, analog laser disk technology had been developed in the 1950s by engineer David Paul Gregg. Russell's earliest patents in the field were issued in the mid-1960s, with a working prototype finished by the 1970s, using a laser to read binary data on a compact disc, or CD. The patent for Russell's invention passed from company to company, bringing him from Richland to Salt Lake City, then to Toronto. His development of the first digital-to-optical recording and playback system became foundational to CD-ROM, DVD and Blu-ray technology. Russell didn't earn royalties, never made a fortune, and was rarely credited for his work.

Other primitive, analog laser disk technology had been developed in the 1950s by engineer David Paul Gregg. Russell's earliest patents in the field were issued in the mid-1960s, with a working prototype finished by the 1970s, using a laser to read binary data on a compact disc, or CD. The patent for Russell's invention passed from company to company, bringing him from Richland to Salt Lake City, then to Toronto. His development of the first digital-to-optical recording and playback system became foundational to CD-ROM, DVD and Blu-ray technology. Russell didn't earn royalties, never made a fortune, and was rarely credited for his work.

Pimalaya goes stable: Himalaya v3 and the pimdir standard
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
09:50:02 UTC
18:50:02 KST

Pimalaya goes stable 🎉
Himalaya v3, Neverest, Cardamum, Calendula and Carillon v1, Ortie and Comodoro v3, all at the top of the first stable version of pimdir. Why everything at once, how it was done, where the wizard went, and what comes next.
It took months, but here we are: the whole Pimalaya CLI family is released at once, at the top of the first stable version of the pimdir standard. Here the list:
- Himalaya v3.0.0, the CLI to manage mails.
- Neverest v1.0.0, the CLI to synchronize mails, contacts and calendars.
- Cardamum v1.0.0, the CLI to manage contacts.
- Calendula v1.0.0, the CLI to manage calendars.
- Carillon v1.0.0, the CLI to watch mailboxes, address books and calendars, and fire hooks.
- Ortie v3.0.0, the CLI to manage OAuth tokens (released 5 days earlier).
- Comodoro v3.0.0, the CLI to manage timers (released 5 days earlier as well).
- pimdir storage v1.0.0, sync v1.0.0 and search v1.0.0, plus its Rust implementation io-pimdir v1.0.0.
Most of them are majors, and they all break something for somebody. This post explains why, how, and where it goes from here. It is long, feel free to jump to the tool you care about.
Why everything at once
In Another year with NLnet I said this year was about consolidation and about a standard for PIM on disk. This release is the result of both.
The thing is, every piece is now connected:
- All the CLIs sit on the same libs (IMAP, SMTP, JMAP, DAV, the Google and Microsoft APIs, OAuth, HTTP, TLS).
- All the CLIs share the same kit: the same configuration loader, the same output contract, the same exit codes, the same REPL and the same MCP server.
- Neverest syncs every account into one pimdir store, and Himalaya, Cardamum, Calendula and Carillon read it, search it and queue their writes into it.
Releasing them one by one meant releasing tools that disagree with each other: one version of the store here, another version of the output there. So I decided to bring every tool to release level first, then to release them all together. It took way longer than I wanted, but this way what you install today is one coherent family.
The other reason is the contract. The CLIs are not only used by humans anymore: they are used by scripts, by frontends (the Vim plugin, the TUI, the Android app), and by AI agents through MCP. All of them need a stable, versioned contract: the same JSON shapes, the same error codes, the same exit codes. Majors are the right moment to fix it, and from now on it should not move anymore without a new major.
How it was done
Few things made this release possible.
Specs before code. Every repository has a cairn/ folder holding its living spec, its change proposals and a dated log. Every behaviour change goes through a proposal, then gets folded into the spec. This way the truth is written somewhere, and not only in my head.
One shared kit. The pimalaya-cli and pimalaya-config libs hold everything the CLIs have in common: the output contract, the error codes, the REPL, the MCP server, the configuration loading. In fact most of the work of this release was moving code from the CLIs into this kit, and deleting the copies.
One unified API, as a union. Before, the commands shared by every backend were the intersection of what the backends could do: if one backend could not do it, nobody could. Now it is the union: each backend registers the capabilities it really serves, with the same observable result as the others, and a command an account cannot serve fails before even connecting, with a clear capability-missing code.
Measured gates for pimdir. Audits always find something, so "no new finding" never ends. Instead, I built a test generator that produces stores, remote states and an oracle from a seed, and ran campaigns of 4,180 jobs against io-pimdir: crashes injected at every step, the search index checked against a full rebuild after each event, the sync engine replayed against scripted servers. The rule was simple: 2 campaigns in a row without any spec break, then freeze. Campaign 1 found one, campaigns 2 and 3 were clean, a late fix restarted the count, then campaigns 4 and 5 were clean. Freeze.
Live tests on real providers. Every CLI was tested against real accounts: Google, Microsoft, Fastmail and iCloud, plus few Chinese providers for Himalaya (QQ, 163, 139). A last "live night" just before the release found few bugs that went straight into the libs.
Libs first, apps after. The libs were released between the 13th and the 25th of November, the apps then moved to them without any patch or git pin. Everything you install comes from crates.io.
pimdir v1, the foundation
pimdir is a local-first standard for personal information: mails, contacts, calendars, tasks and files in one portable store, kept as a replica of every source, and searchable with one query language. A store is one SQLite file plus a content-addressed blob directory, the same on every OS and architecture. I already explained why such a standard is needed in Another year with NLnet, and how the Android app became its first full implementation in Pimalaya for Android enters closed alpha.
It comes in 3 parts, versioned separately:
- Storage: the store itself (schema, blobs, summaries, change feed, retention, action queue).
- Sync: how sources are reconciled through the store (merge rules, the connector seam).
- Search: the derived index and the query language.
They are released together because a store holds sync state that follows the sync part, and the query language binds every program that offers search.
What v1 means: schema version 1 never changes, every schema change is a migration, and a minor version only adds test cases. You can implement it in any language that speaks SQLite, use the I/O-free core of io-pimdir (like the Android app does, on the platform SQLite), or use its std client.
Few days before the freeze, I compared it with notmuch, which is the reference in this area. They do not do the same job (notmuch indexes mail files it does not own, pimdir owns the bodies, syncs them and holds other domains), but the search can be compared. On a store of 1 million messages from the Enron corpus, pimdir was ahead of notmuch on 50 of the 51 query measures and tied the last one, imported the million faster, and used a third of the memory. Stemming, regex and per-thread queries are still missing, they are on the list.
One warning for the early adopters: a store written by a draft version is refused, never migrated. Sync it till nothing is pending with the last draft binary, move its directory aside, then let a release create and sync a new one.
Useful links:
- pimdir repository, with STORAGE.md, SYNC.md, SEARCH.md and the informative OVERVIEW.md
- pimdir changelog
- io-pimdir repository, release notes and API documentation
Where did the wizard go?
This is the change many of you will not like, so let me explain it properly.
Every CLI had a configuration wizard: you typed your email address, it discovered the servers, asked for the password, stored it in your keyring, and wrote the TOML for you. It is gone, from every CLI.
Why. Few reasons, piled up:
- It was already broken. For Gmail, Microsoft and JMAP, the Himalaya wizard handed the sign-in to ortie configure, which Ortie v3 removed. The path it proposed first for the most common providers simply failed.
- It cost a lot. The Himalaya wizard alone was 14 files and about 4,000 lines, and 1.90 MB of a 20.3 MB binary. Same story in each CLI: server discovery, a keyring dialogue, a review screen and a TOML writer, inside tools whose job is to manage mails, contacts or calendars.
- It served 2 audiences badly. Power users write their config from the sample anyway. People who need a guided setup are better served by an app than by a prompt in a terminal.
- The CLIs moved to plain text and JSON, with no terminal handling at all. A wizard is exactly the kind of interactive layer that does not fit anymore.
What replaces it.
- A first-run welcome. Run a CLI without configuration, and it names the missing file and the sample to start from. At a terminal, it even prints the exact commands to copy the sample in place, then the first command to run.
- A tested config.sample.toml, shipped in the packages and the release archives under share/doc. The samples are parsed by the test suites, so they cannot silently rot anymore.
- Ortie for OAuth, with ready-made Google, Microsoft and Fastmail accounts to paste from its README, using Pimalaya's own clients.
- pim-discovery, for the discovery part of the wizard. It is the CLI of io-pim-discovery, the lib the wizards used (and the Android app still uses): pim-discovery all user@fastmail.com finds the IMAP, SMTP, JMAP, CalDAV and CardDAV servers of an address, and tells you what vouches for each answer. It is not officially released yet, but you can install it with cargo install io-pim-discovery --locked --features cli.
- Guided setup in frontends. The Android app keeps its onboarding (you type your email address, it finds the rest), and Himalaya TUI has a draft to bring the same flow to the terminal.
The direction. The CLIs are now low-level tools: plain text to glance at, JSON for scripts, frontends and agents. Comfort (tables, colours, relative dates, guided setups, rich composition) belongs to frontends. In fact this is not new: it is the same split that started the whole project, back when the logic left the Vim plugin. The CLI was always supposed to be the backend a frontend needs. This release just goes all the way.
I know some of you used the wizard to get started, and that copying a sample feels like a step back. If the welcome or the samples are not clear enough, it is a bug: please open an issue, I would rather fix the docs than bring back a second setup system per CLI.
Himalaya v3
Himalaya is now a low-level CLI over a unified mail API: the same commands on every backend, the protocol-specific ones next to them, plain text for a glance and --json for the rest.
Backends: IMAP, SMTP, ManageSieve, JMAP, the Gmail API, Microsoft Graph, Maildir (with a new maildir++ layout for Dovecot and Courier stores), mbox, and pimdir. The README has recipes for Proton Mail (through Bridge), Fastmail, Gmail, Outlook, Posteo and iCloud.
What is new:
- himalaya repl, one command per line, with clients connected once for the whole session.
- himalaya mcp, an MCP server over stdio. Writes are refused unless you pass --allow-write, and sending is behind its own --allow-send.
- The pimdir query language for envelope search on every backend (from:alice date:2d).
- vacation get|set on JMAP and Gmail, message reply --all, mutual TLS, an account-level tls table.
- A stable code on every failure, an errors list per item, and a JSON Schema for every command.
What is gone: the wizard, the m2dir backend (superseded by pimdir), the table and colour options, HIMALAYA_CONFIG. Unknown configuration keys are now refused, JSON keys are camelCase, and the v2 search syntax needs to be rewritten. The migration guide lists everything, with a full table for the queries.
Useful links:
Neverest v1
Neverest is rebuilt around the pimdir store: every account syncs into one shared store, which the other CLIs read, search and queue their writes into. It syncs mails, contacts and calendars over IMAP, Gmail, Microsoft Graph, Google Calendar, Google People, CalDAV and CardDAV.
Highlights:
- Two-way, one-way or migration syncs.
- Three-way merge of cards and events: a real conflict is parked for you to decide, never lost.
- Retention: a remote deletion never removes the local copy, only an explicit purge does.
- Writes queued by Himalaya, Cardamum and Calendula are applied offline, sending included.
- Paged, newest-first listings that resume after an interruption, back-off on throttling, and a clean stop on Ctrl-C.
JMAP sources are not supported yet (it is a draft), so Fastmail syncs over IMAP, CardDAV and CalDAV for now. Coming from v0.x? Your configuration and state are not carried over: start from the config.sample.toml, then run neverest sync --init.
Useful links:
Cardamum v1
The first stable release of Cardamum: a low-level CLI over a unified contacts API, with the same commands on CardDAV, JMAP, Microsoft Graph, Google People, vdir and pimdir.
card search speaks the pimdir query language on every backend, account capability shows what an account serves without connecting, and you get the REPL and the MCP server like the others. Editing a card is left to a composer such as tcard. The wizard and the discover route are gone.
Useful links:
- Repository
- Release notes
- Changelog
- Sample configuration
- tcard, to compose cards
Calendula v1
Same story for calendars: the same commands on CalDAV, JMAP (new), Google Calendar, Microsoft Graph, vdir and pimdir.
New: event search, recurrence expansion, attendees and alarms, online meetings (--online-meeting), invitation replies through pimdir, and an agenda that draws a month or a year like cal(1) does. Editing an event is left to a composer such as tcal. The wizard, the tables and colours, and the todo and journal commands are gone: tasks and journals will move to dedicated tools (Unfog and Journal), not released yet.
Useful links:
- Repository
- Release notes
- Changelog
- Sample configuration
- tcal, to compose events
Carillon v1
Carillon is a local watcher that fires hooks on what changes in your mailboxes, address books and calendars, over IMAP, JMAP, Maildir, CalDAV, CardDAV, Microsoft Graph, the Google APIs and the pimdir store. It uses push where the server offers it (IMAP IDLE, the JMAP event stream) and polls elsewhere. A hook runs a command, shows a desktop notification, or both.
New in v1: a pimdir backend, on-alarm-trigger for the alarms of events and tasks, on-birthday-trigger for birthdays and anniversaries, a per-hook timeout, and a report of what moved while an IMAP or Gmail session was lost. Hooks now take the same shape as Comodoro's.
Useful links:
Ortie v3
Ortie manages OAuth tokens for the other tools. v3 aligns it on the rest of the family: ready-made Google, Microsoft and Fastmail accounts to paste, token revocation, an auth listen to catch a redirection handed off from another machine, account checks, a REPL, and an MCP server that never hands the token to the agent. The wizard and the hooks are gone (the storage write command does what a hook did).
Useful links:
Comodoro v3
Comodoro manages timers: one server owns a timer, any number of clients drive it, over a local socket or TCP. v3 joins the same standard: comodoro watch feeds a status bar without polling, hooks get the cycle name and duration, plus the welcome, account checks, a REPL and an MCP server. Comodoro is now a binary only: the library is gone, and the open JSON-RPC protocol is the contract for third parties.
Useful links:
The libs
None of the above would exist without the libs below, all released in November on crates.io:
- The shared kit: pimalaya-cli v0.8, pimalaya-config v0.6, pimalaya-stream v0.4.
- The content models: mime-rs v0.2, vcard-rs v0.6, ical-rs v0.7.
- The protocols: io-imap v0.12, io-smtp v0.10, io-sasl v0.3, io-http v0.9, io-jmap v0.11, io-webdav v0.14, io-managesieve v0.6, io-oauth v0.8, io-proxy v0.3, io-pim-discovery v0.15.
- The provider APIs: io-gmail v0.10, io-msgraph v0.11, io-gcal v0.6, io-gpeople v0.9, io-gtasks v0.3.
- The local formats: io-maildir v0.5, io-mbox v0.2, io-vdir v0.3, and io-pimdir v1.0.
They are all I/O-free, as usual: feel free to build your own tools at the top of them.
Installing and upgrading
Each README lists the ways to install: prebuilt binaries from the GitHub releases (or the install.sh script), cargo install --locked, Nix, and the APT, DNF and Homebrew repositories at pkg.pimalaya.org. The packages are landing there over the coming days, and the nixpkgs and home-manager updates are on their way.
About upgrading:
- Himalaya, Ortie and Comodoro have a MIGRATION.md, follow it.
- Neverest, Cardamum, Calendula and Carillon come from v0.x: there is no migration guide, start from the config.sample.toml.
- pimdir stores written by a draft are refused, see the procedure above.
What comes next
- Neverest v1.0.1, with few fixes already on their way, then JMAP sources.
- Himalaya TUI, with the onboarding of the Android app.
- The Android app, already moved to pimdir sync v1 and io-pimdir v1.0.
- pimdir search: stemming, regex, per-thread queries, local labels and properties.
- Tasks and journals, through Unfog and Journal.
- Packaging: nixpkgs, home-manager and pkg.pimalaya.org for every tool.
Thank you
Special thanks to the NLnet foundation and the European Commission's NGI initiative, for the financial support through NGI Zero Core and now the NGI Zero Commons Fund. This kind of slow, deep work is only possible thanks to them.
And thank you to everyone who reported bugs, tested betas, or simply waited patiently while everything was being rebuilt. Any feedback is welcomed, on GitHub, on Matrix or on Mastodon. Let me know how it goes!

Millions lose access to online benefits and pension services after 'cyber incident'published at 18:40 GMT
- 18:40 GMT
- Jamie Whitehead Live reporter
- Image source, PA Media
- Millions of people haven't been able to access online benefits and pension services, as the government says its system has been hit by what it calls a "cyber incident".
- The Department for Work and Pensions (DWP) says its digital services - including for pension, universal credit and Pip applications - were taken down as a "precaution", while assuring claimants regular payments would be paid.
- Downing Street hasn't ruled out "malicious" activity, and says there is no evidence of data loss in the incident, with investigations into the cause ongoing.
- Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden described the incident as "serious", with the DWP working with the National Cyber Security Centre to get things running again.
On any particular day, there can be problems with processing some paymentspublished at 18:29 GMT
- 18:29 GMT
- Michael Buchanan Social affairs correspondent
- The DWP processes around 2.5 million payments each day, worth about £1.3bn.
- On any particular day, there are problems with a small proportion of them.
- And while it’s logical that anyone who has a problem today is linking it to the cyber incident, the DWP are emphasising that they currently have no information to suggest regular payments are being affected.
- They also say that anyone who fears a benefit sanction as a result of being unable to upload information to their digital journal today should not worry as they will not be penalised due to the DWP suspending their online services.
'Focus on readiness', but it's 'a way of life' - the reaction we've heardpublished at 18:21 GMT
- 18:21 GMT
- Image source, Reuters
- The DWP said it took down some online services as a "precaution" after identifying the "cyber incident". Here is the response:
- From those in the field:
- Haroon Malik, a fellow at the Chartered Institute of Information Security, said it's "very uncommon" for governments to be taken offline by cyber incidents and it needs to focus on "readiness" and accept that this is going to happen "more and more".
- Trevor Dearing, a senior director at cybersecurity company Illumio, said the longer-term goal should be tighter controls: "Millions rely on DWP services, and the goal must be that an attack happens and the public doesn't notice."
- Beverley Griffiths, senior lecturer in security and resilience at Buckinghamshire New University says cyber incidents are now "a way of our life", but that the public should be reassured "resilience plans" are in place.
- And those who rely on the services:
- Meanwhile, some people have expressed frustration about services being taken down - Craig in Scotland told the BBC he was offered a trial job tomorrow but cannot get a suit, tie and shoes without his advance universal credit payment.
- Meanwhile single mum-of-four Samantha has said she relies on the payment and has "just enough money in my account to do a food shop".
- The DWP has said that regular benefit and pension payments have not been affected by the cyber incident.
- This is a service that lets you report a death to most government organisations in one go.
- It informs HM Revenue & Customs (HMRC), the Department for Work and Pensions (DWP), the Passport Office, the Driver and Vehicle Licensing Agency (DVLA), the local council and more.
- The reason for the outage seems to be because "Tell Us Once" is managed by the DWP, despite connecting with many other government departments.
- When you try to use the service online this afternoon, the DWP outage message appears.
Cyber incidents now 'a way of our life', security and resilience lecturer sayspublished at 17:10 GMT
- 17:10 GMT
- An expert in security and resilience says the UK will likely continue to see cyber incidents occur.
- "This is the world we live in now," Beverley Griffiths, senior lecturer in security and resilience at Buckinghamshire New University, tells the Press Association.
- "And you know they [the public] should take comfort in the fact that these departments, these organisations have got resilience plans in place - they are responding."
- Griffiths doesn't think the public should be too worried, but says "there is a lot going on in the world at the moment".
- She feels the "more complex we are", the more gaps and weaknesses we have, adding that it is almost like living with the weather.
- "It is a way of our life," Griffiths adds.
- Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden describes the outage as a "serious cyber incident", with investigations into the cause ongoing - Downing Street has not ruled out "malicious" activity.
What are the DWP's online services used for?published at 16:48 GMT
- 16:48 GMT
- Image source, Geography Photos via Getty Images
- Let's take a look at universal credit, as an example. Recipients can use their online account to:
- See when their next payment is due and how much they'll get
- Send messages to their work coach or case manager
- Report changes in their circumstances to ensure they receive the correct amount
- Receive notification of appointments - including where and when to attend
- Inform the DWP what they are doing to find work by recording information in the online journal
- The issue is broader than just universal credit, though:
- When you try to use the DWP website to see if you can apply for other benefits like personal independence payments (PIP), it takes you through to an error page which flags "temporary disruption"
- The same is true when you click on the application page for Jobseeker's Allowance
- If you've reached the age where you can first apply to receive state pension, you can usually do that using the DWP website too. But anyone clicking the "apply now" button will also find themselves landing on an error page

- "So I rely on the payment today - it's my payday."
- She says that she was told by the DWP adviser that the payment could come into her account by 20:00 GMT today at the latest, but that if she still hasn't received it she will have to wait until Monday.
- "I can't wait and see," Samantha says. "Even my Tesco mobile has bounced today so I don't have enough mobile data.
- "I'm panicking - it's going to be a long and stressful weekend."
- The DWP have told the BBC regular payments are not affected and that if customers have any issues with their payments, they should call on their benefit phoneline.
- The NCSC leads the UK's defence against cyber threats and helps individuals, businesses and public sector organisations protect their online services and devices.
- It offers proactive guidance for defending against such threats and also provides incident response when they do occur.
- This can include determining the extent of the incident, managing its impact, restoring systems and working to increase security to prevent it happening again.
- The NCSC is part of the Government Communications Headquarters (GCHQ) and works closely with international allies, law enforcement and the UK’s intelligence and security agencies, including MI5 and MI6.
More than 15,000 reports of gov.uk problems since yesterday eveningpublished at 15:41 GMT
- 15:41 GMT
- By Kayleen Devlin
- More than 15,000 reports of problems with the gov.uk website were submitted to outage monitoring website Downdetector between yesterday evening and lunchtime today.
- Downdetector tells BBC Verify that more than 1,300 reports about the UK government’s website were received between 19:20 and 21:00 GMT yesterday. Typically, they would expect around one report or fewer every 15 minutes.
- While Downdetector does not track individual government departments and cannot establish how many complaints related specifically to DWP services, it represents a significant surge of reports on the gov.uk domain over an extended period of time.
- Around two-thirds concerned login problems, with reports coming from across the UK.
- A DWP spokesperson tells BBC Verify that the department became aware of the cyber incident on Thursday, but declined to give an exact time.
- The spokesperson says there is currently no evidence of personal data being compromised or payments being affected. Whether anyone gained unauthorised access to DWP systems remains under investigation.
What to know about the Department for Work and Pensions cyber incidentpublished at 15:25 GMT

- 'Serious cyber incident': The Department for Work and Pensions has been hit by a "serious cyber incident" but there's no evidence anyone's data has been compromised, according to Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden.
- Who or what is to blame? We don't yet know what's behind the outage, but the prime minister's spokesperson says there is currently "no evidence to suggest a malicious actor has targeted other government departments".

- 'Done the right thing': Despite the inconvenience for people seeking to use the DWP site, some experts say taking it offline was the correct protocol to stop a potential attack from escalating.
- The DWP has "extremely old computer systems": According to its most senior official Sarah Healey, who told a Parliamentary committee earlier this month that the department's cyber-security and resilience measures are "critical".
Sanctions will be waived if you’re affected todaypublished at 14:58 GMT

Cyber incident or cyber attack - what's the difference?published at 14:21 GMT
- 14:21 GMT
- Joe Tidy Cyber correspondent
- The Department for Work and Pensions says it is dealing with a "cyber incident" and is not saying much else about what’s happening inside the IT networks of this vast government department.
- The term has become a catch-all one used frequently in the early stages of what often ends up being a cyber attack.
- The reason organisations often use the term "cyber incident" rather than "cyber attack" or "data breach" is that in the opening stages of an incident they may not yet know:
- Whether an attacker successfully gained access
- Whether data was stolen
- Whether the event was malicious, accidental, or merely suspicious activity
- How widespread the impact is
- From a communications perspective, "cyber incident" is also often the safest legally and technically accurate phrase while teams try to figure out what’s going on.
- As a reminder, an investigation is under way into the incident. Downing Street has said that "currently there is no evidence of customer data loss".
Protecting public is 'constant challenge for government' - Pat McFaddenpublished at 14:06 GMT
- 14:06 GMT
- Image source, UK Pool
- A bit more from Pat McFadden's update on the cyber incident at the DWP.
- Asked about comments from the chair of the Science and Technology Committee (which you can read in our previous post) that the disruption should be "a wake up call", McFadden says they are "constantly working to protect our systems".
- "When we get an incident like this, the most important thing to do is to protect the public," he says, adding protecting the public's data is a "constant challenge for government".
- McFadden adds working to secure key systems is a "critical part of the UK's resilience".

Lego Project Hail Mary set
Outside of the Integrity, few spacecraft made as big a splash in 2026 as the one Ryland Grace piloted in Project Hail Mary. And now, thanks to Lego, you can construct a microscale replica of the titular Hail Mary, complete with a miniature Rocky figure.
Hoto 3.6V Electric Screwdriver Kit Pro
Hoto’s rechargeable screwdriver is ideal for small repairs. It offers three torque modes, a rotational speed of up to 220RPM, and a helpful circular LED to illuminate dark spaces. The durable case also holds all 25 steel bits and an extension bar for getting into hard-to-reach places.
Sony DualSense Wireless Controller
Sony’s DualSense Wireless Controller for the PlayStation 5 features immersive haptic feedback and resistance triggers, which feel great to use. It offers a built-in mic, a 3.5mm headphone jack, and a mute button; it also works with PC, macOS, and mobile devices via Bluetooth or USB-C.
MacBook Neo
Apple’s most affordable laptop uses the same A18 Pro chip found in the iPhone 16 Pro. It features a vibrant 13-inch display, surprisingly good speakers, and 8GB of RAM, making it a solid option for students and anyone who needs a laptop for everyday tasks. Read our review.
Ember Mug 3 (14-ounce)
Ember’s latest smart mug is designed to keep your coffee at the ideal temperature for up to three hours, with five temp settings between 120 and 145 degrees Fahrenheit. The third-gen version is similar to prior models; however, you can now adjust the heat settings directly on the mug and throw it in the dishwasher, which wasn’t the case before.
LG B6E OLED TV
Despite being an entry-level variant of the B6 OLED, this model features four HDMI 2.1 ports, a 120Hz refresh rate, a capable processor, and pixel-perfect image quality and contrast for any content you want to watch. Lower brightness is likely the biggest compromise with getting a cheaper OLED.
Alienware 32-inch 4K OLED gaming monitor
One of the most affordable Tandem RGB Stripe OLEDs is even cheaper during Black Friday. The AW3226Q monitor has a 165Hz refresh rate, G-Sync compatibility, better-looking text rendering than most other OLED monitors, and unbeatable contrast.
JBL Tour One M3
JBL’s Tour One M3 noise-canceling headphones come with a touchscreen-equipped wireless transmitter that lets you wirelessly listen to audio from in-flight entertainment systems and other devices without Bluetooth. They also offer solid active noise cancellation and last up to 70 hours on a charge.
Apple Watch Ultra 4
The latest super-rugged wearable from Apple features better battery life than the previous version, plus AI-powered features like Siri Recap also available on the Series 12.
Iniu P55-E2 Power Bank (10,000mAh)
Iniu’s P55-E2 Power Bank is an ultra-slim three-port USB charger that’s capable of fully recharging an iPhone 16 twice. It supports charging speeds of up to 45W via a USB-C port, which can deliver fast recharging for a range of gadgets.
Hisense CanvasTV (2026)
Hisense’s 2026 CanvasTV is a 4K QLED TV that doubles as wall art when you’re not watching. It features a matte, anti-glare display and runs on Google’s excellent TV software; it’s also a great pick for gamers thanks to its snappy 144Hz variable refresh rate and a pair of HDMI 2.1 ports.
Elegoo Centauri Carbon 2 Combo
The Elegoo Centauri Carbon 2 Combo is a beginner-friendly 3D printer that supports four filament spools. It features an enclosed build chamber, automatic bed leveling, and a large touchscreen, making it easy to produce reliable prints.
Moft Trackable Tripod Wallet
Moft’s Trackable Tripod Wallet is a MagSafe-compatible wallet that holds two cards and folds into an adjustable phone stand or small tripod. It also supports both Apple’s Find My network and Google’s Find Hub, offers Qi wireless charging, and includes a detachable shutter remote for taking photos.
Birdfy Feeder Metal 2 4K
Birdfy’s metal smart bird feeder features a wide-angle 4K camera that lets you get an up-close look at visiting birds, along with a 1.8-liter seed capacity and a built-in solar panel for charging. It also offers free AI-powered recognition of more than 6,000 bird species.
Sharge Icemag 3 Power Bank
The Icemag 3 can wirelessly deliver up to 25W of power to compatible smartphones and up to 35W wired charging via USB-C. The 10,000mAh Qi2 charger can also act as a foldable kickstand, comes with an integrated USB-C cable that serves as a lanyard, and sports a transparent design with colorful RGB lighting.
Saros
Saros is a third-person sci-fi shooter in which players fight alien enemies on a mysterious planet trapped in a time loop. The PS5 exclusive features fast-paced combat, a variety of powerful weapons, and challenging boss fights. Read our review.
Bose QuietComfort Headphones Gen 2
Bose’s second-gen QuietComfort Headphones build on the original with better sound quality and support for lossless audio over USB-C. They also offer improved noise cancellation and are more comfortable thanks to their lack of “eardrum suck.”
Dreame X60 Ultra
The Dreame X60 Ultra is a powerful robot vacuum and mop that does a great job of cleaning carpets thanks to its dual roller brushes and 35,000Pa of suction. It can climb over tall thresholds, squeeze under low furniture, and automatically detach its mop pads when vacuuming carpets.
Garmin Forerunner 265S
Garmin’s midrange Forerunner watch has an OLED display, a week’s worth of battery life, and dual-frequency GPS for better accuracy. Read our review.
Samsung The Frame (2025)
Samsung’s last-gen Frame is a 4K QLED TV that displays art on its anti-glare matte screen when not in use. It also supports Dolby Atmos, HDR10 Plus, and popular streaming apps, with refresh rates up to 144Hz on larger models.
TP-Link Tapo Matter-Certified Smart Plug Mini
TP-Link’s Wi-Fi smart plug lets you remotely control lamps, fans, and other appliances using your phone or voice commands. It supports Matter, so it works with all major smart home platforms to set schedules and timers to automatically turn devices on or off.
Pixel Buds 2a
Google’s latest pair of wireless earbuds features crisp sound, a comfortable design, and solid active noise cancellation, which is great considering the budget-friendly price tag. They even offer built-in support for Gemini and, unlike the entry-level AirPods, a purple option! Read our review.
Roku Pro Series 4K OLED TV
Roku now makes some of the most affordable, yet feature-packed 4K OLED TVs available for gamers or movie lovers looking for a big TV upgrade under $1,000. The Pro Series LX with better brightness, faster refresh rate, and a polarizer to reduce reflections starts at $200 more.
Alienware AW2726DM QD-OLED monitor
If you’re on a tight budget, but want spectacular contrast and brightness control that only an OLED can provide, here’s a deal for you. Alienware’s 1440p 27-inch QD-OLED monitor with 240Hz refresh rate is selling at its lowest price yet. Read our hands-on.
Dell XPS 13 (2026)
The best XPS 13 in years. It doesn’t have a headphone jack and starts with only 8GB of RAM but it’s an excellent thin-and-light with a great screen, a solid Intel chip, and speedy storage.
Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro
Soundcore makes some of the best wireless earbuds you’ve never heard of — and the Liberty 5 Pro may be its best pair yet. The sleek buds deliver powerful noise cancellation, good sound, and surprisingly fabulous call quality for less than Apple’s latest AirPods.
Beats Powerbeats Pro 2
The latest Powerbeats Pro are a no-brainer for athletes. They pack fantastic sound and thumping bass, along with active noise cancellation, IPX4 water resistance, and heart rate monitoring. Read our review.
Logitech G Pro X2 Superstrike
The big difference between the Superstrike and other gaming mice lies beneath its main two mouse buttons. It uses haptic actuators instead of mechanical switches, allowing for lower latency and more longevity.
Yoto Mini (2024) speaker
A great first portable music player/speaker for kids. It uses insertable NFC cards (sold separately) to play music and stories to keep kiddos occupied and entertained without zoning out in front of the TV. This model supports Bluetooth and 3.5mm for private listening, too.
Nex Playground
The Nex Playground’s motion-controlled gameplay is designed for ages five and up. It comes with games like Fruit Ninja and Whack-a-Mole Deluxe, but many more titles are available via a subscription. It’s a ton of fun for kids. This is the lowest price so far in 2026. Read our review.
Apple AirPods Max (second-gen)
Nothing has changed in terms of aesthetics, but the Max 2 deliver better sound and some new features. Not exactly worth upgrading for, but fantastic for those who are considering them now. Read our full review.
Govee Uplighter Floor Lamp
It’s hard to find a cooler lamp than Govee’s Uplighter floor lamp at this price. It can light a room, and display a mesmerizing pattern up your wall and onto your ceiling.
Meta Quest 3S
The Meta Quest 3S is a battery-powered standalone VR headset that has the same chipset as the pricier Quest 3, but with the same lenses and screen resolution as the Quest 2. In addition to being able to play many games from the Meta Store, it can also be connected to a gaming PC to play VR games like Half-Life Alyx. Read our review.
Ghost of Yōtei
Sucker Punch’s follow-up to Ghost of Tsushima is everything you could want in a sequel. The open-world revenge epic brims with fluid combat and a host of welcome narrative twists, all set against the gorgeous backdrop of Japan’s Edo period. It’s precisely the type of game photo modes were made for, even if you never use them.
Beats 360 headphones
The Beats 360 are ANC headphones for people who prefer headphones over earbuds at the gym. They’re Beats’ first IP-rated cans, with ear cushions that you can easily remove for cleaning (or style points). They also stay in place during most exercises, while still delivering solid bass. Read our review.
Donkey Kong Bananza
Donkey Kong Bananza is one of the Switch 2’s first exclusive must-have titles. Its destructible environment allows you to smash your way through each of its 17 levels, and the paths you make will remain intact unless you specifically choose to reset the world. Your companion Paulene’s singing allows you to transform into animals that have abilities that allow you to reach previously inaccessible parts of levels. Read our review.
Lego Mandalorian N-1 Starfighter
Arguably the best thing to come out of The Book of Boba Fett was the Mandalorian’s N-1 Starfighter, which Lego has recreated here in miniature. It’s not quite as impressive as the 1,802-piece version, but the cheaper set still packs plenty of detail, including a spring-loaded shooter.
The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time
Nintendo’s latest Switch 2 exclusive is the best way to relive what is arguably the best Zelda game of all time. The revamped visuals look gorgeous, and while the game’s improved controls might annoy some purists who still cling to the heyday of ’98, we reckon most folks won’t bat an eye.
Amazon Alexa Photo Frame
A 10-inch anti-glare touchscreen that comes in a few design options. It’s a frame that features Alexa, and doubles as a smart speaker. It works with Amazon Photos, which offers unlimited storage for Prime members.
Aqara Smart Lock U400
This innovative new smart lock uses the U1 ultra-wideband (UWB) chip in many iPhones and Apple Watches to automatically unlock your door as you approach. It also works with the new Aliro standard, making it compatible with Android phones for hands-free unlocking. Read our review.
Twelve South AirFly SE
Twelve South’s entry-level Bluetooth transmitter delivers up to 25 hours of continuous use and lets you connect up to two pairs of headphones to an in-flight entertainment system, making it a great travel companion no matter where you’re going.
Catan (6th Edition)
In Catan, up to four players strategically compete and trade for resources. The iconic board game ends when one player reaches 10 “victory points,” which is achieved by constructing a sprawling civilization of roads, settlements, and cities using sheep, ore, wheat, and wood.
Control Resonant
Control Resonant is all about manipulation — of gravity, not people. Remedy’s excellent sequel to Control is an exhilarating action game, with ever-changing combat, twisted level design, and the weirdest traversal mechanics we’ve seen in a long time.
Birdbuddy Pro (solar)
Birdbuddy’s charming, camera-equipped feeder identifies and snaps shots of each bird that stops by, supplementing its 5-megapixel stills with insightful facts via an app. The solar roof helps keep the camera’s battery topped up even when it’s cloudy outside.
Azul
Azul is a game in which you arrange patterned tiles in order to create the most beautiful tapestry possible. Arranging tiles in a strategic sequence will help you build your score while limiting your opponents’ ability to grow theirs.
Magna-Tile Classic 32 Piece Set
Magna-Tiles are a quintessential kids’ toy. The colorful, semi-translucent geometric shapes are a favorite here at The Verge, especially for those of us who prefer the simplicity of building with magnets rather than bricks.
Lego Botanicals Mini Bonsai Trees set
Lego’s 709-piece kit lets you construct three brick-ified bonsai trees, including a black pine, a ginkgo with yellow leaves, and a wisteria with hanging lilac flowers. It also comes with three ornamental displays, just in case you want a raised stand on which to showcase them.
Sonos Beam (Gen 2)
The Beam isn’t as powerful as the Sonos Arc, nor can it match the flagship soundbar’s Dolby Atmos immersion bubble. But you still get room-filling surround sound for less money — and the Beam ties into the Sonos ecosystem for added convenience. Read our review.
Google Pixel 11 Pro
Google wants to sell you on the Pro’s software features, but it’s the underlying hardware that makes it a stellar Android handset. The build quality is incredible, the larger telephoto sensor is an improvement, and it now supports faster wired speeds and Qi2.2 for up to 25W wireless charging. Read our review.
Roborock Qrevo Curv 2 Flow
Roborock’s first roller-mop robot vacuum, the Curv 2 Flow, features flagship specs at a midrange price, including excellent mopping, a retractable mop cover, 20,000Pa suction, AI-powered obstacle avoidance, and a compact design. Read our review.
Insta360 Link 2
The Insta360 Link 2 offers the same auto-swiveling design as its excellent predecessor, only in a smaller form factor. It also offers a slate of new features, including group tracking and no-follow zones.
CMF Buds 2A
With 12.4mm drivers, active noise cancellation, and an IP54 rating, the CMF Buds 2A offer high-end features at an affordable price. You can also customize the sound, adjust ANC levels, and find the earbuds using the Nothing X app.
Philips Hue Festavia string lights
These smart lights have beautiful colors, a nice selection of festive scenes, and an easy-to-use app. They are super reliable when paired with a Hue Bridge and also work over Bluetooth. They come in three lengths, work with all the major smart home platforms, and are Matter-compatible. Read our review.
Echo Dot Max
The Echo Dot Max features a flat design and a redesigned sound system, which delivers nearly three times the bass as its predecessor. It also offers improved conversation detection and better noise filtering, thanks to Amazon’s new AZ3 chip. Read our review.
Glocusent Bookmark Style Reading Light
Glocusent’s clip-in reading light features five brightness levels and multiple color temperature settings, ranging from a soft, warm glow that’s great for late-night reading to a brighter light that’s perfect for daytime use. The slim USB light easily slips into your pocket, too, so you can read anywhere, from a cozy bed to a dimly lit train.
Vantage
Vantage isn’t your typical open-world exploration game. It’s a sci-fi roguelike that has you traversing an alien planet alongside up to five players, each of whom must solve puzzles and complete their mission before their time, morale, or health runs out. It’s not as charming as Stonemaier’s award-winning Wingspan, but it is far more interesting.
SwitchBot Standing Circulator Fan
A quiet, battery-powered fan that moves a lot of air despite its small size, the Switchbot Standing Circulator is a surprisingly versatile smart home addition.
JBL Go 4
The last-gen JBL Go 4 has been around for a bit, but the pocket-sized Bluetooth speaker still delivers with up to seven hours of runtime, dual-device connectivity, and an IP67 rating for water and dust resistance. You can also pair it with other Go 4 speakers for bigger stereo sound, if desired.
Fairphone 6 Plus
Fairphone’s latest is a very light update to the 6, adding a newer processor and carrying forward its repairability and ethical values. Tradeoffs compared to a traditional smartphone are few, and the benefits are real.
Ugreen Nexode 240W USB-C Cable with Digital Display
The proliferation of miniature screens continues with Ugreen’s latest Nexode USB-C Cable, which features a full-color LCD screen on one end that displays current power draw (up to 240W). It also supports 480Mbps data transfer speeds and, unlike similar power meter-equipped cables, lets you turn off the screen when needed.
Apple Watch SE 3
The Apple Watch SE 3 is a major glow-up, adding an always-on display, wrist-temperature sensor, a more powerful processor, and fast charging. Read our review.
Pokémon Pokopia
Nintendo’s latest Pokémon spinoff for the Switch 2 is part Pokémon, part Minecraft, and part Animal Crossing. Yet, despite borrowing gameplay mechanics from each, the cozy life sim is more than the sum of its expansive, building-centric parts. It’s breezy and warm-hearted, with enough secrets to keep mother dearest occupied for hours on end.
Google Pixel 10A
Google’s new Pixel 10A introduces faster wired charging, Satellite SOS, and new AI tools like Camera Coach and Auto Best Take. Otherwise, it’s basically the Pixel 9A, with the same Tensor G4 chip and a dual-camera system. Read our review.
Asus ProArt P14 (RTX Spark)
The entry-point into RTX Spark laptops, the Asus ProArt is a creator-focused MacBook Pro wannabe with a sleek blacked-out design and lovely OLED display. Read our initial Nvidia RTX Spark hands-on.
Fractal Scape gaming headset
There are many gaming headsets out there, but few as elegant as Fractal’s Scape. The PC-focused (but Switch- and PlayStation-compatible) wireless headset offer great sound and include a wireless charger, and they have easy-to-master physical controls.
Nintendo eShop gift cards
Everyone knows gift cards aren’t the best gift, but with such steep discounts happening on Nintendo eShop credit, you can save a lot on a Switch 2, or games and accessories, if you’re alright with jumping through a couple extra hoops.
Xreal One Pro AR glasses
USB-C display glasses can make travel (or relaxing on the couch) more fun by putting a huge virtual screen in front of your face. Xreal’s One Pro are some of the best out there at displaying content from your phone, tablet, laptop, or portable handheld.
Govee Permanent Outdoor Lights 2
Govee’s Permanent Outdoor Lights 2 are Matter-ready smart lights you can leave up year-round. They can display multiple colors at once, with 100 preset lighting scenes, customizable effects, and the ability to sync to music. The lights also feature an IP67 rating for dust and water resistance.
Ecobee Smart Thermostat Premium
The Ecobee Smart Thermostat Premium is an excellent option for adding smarts and some style to your HVAC system. As well as being a smart way to control your energy use, the thermostat can also act as a video intercom for Ecobee’s excellent doorbell camera and an indoor air quality monitor. Read our review.
Update, November 27th: Updated to include several new picks, including those for Govee’s Permanent Outdoor Lights 2 and the Ecobee Smart Thermostat Premium.

European Commission Declares Browser Competition a Digital Markets Act Priority
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
08:11:46 UTC
17:11:46 KST

In comments to Politico regarding our open letter, the European Commission acknowledged the letter and noted that browsers are “effectively the entry point to the web” and one of the main ways publishers reach readers. They went on to say that “browser competition is one of the priorities of the Digital Markets Act”. The EU Commission also stated it is in talks to ensure rival browsers “can effectively compete” with Safari on iPhones and iPads.
Share and join the conversation: Mastodon, LinkedIn, Bluesky and X.
An Extraordinary Coalition in Defence of the Web
The letter has garnered incredible support. We want to say a massive thank you to all the organisations, businesses and individuals that have stepped forward to defend the web's ability to compete fairly as an open platform for consumers and businesses alike.
What is particularly extraordinary is the sheer diversity of the coalition, ranging from human rights, consumer rights, software freedom advocacy organisations to Europe's major publishing associations, prominent startup organisations, independent browser vendors and technology companies.
The breadth of this support demonstrates that browser and web app competition is not simply an issue for browser vendors, but one that affects the entire digital economy and society more broadly.
Signatories to the letter include, among many others:
Civil Society, Consumer Rights and Software Freedom
- Open Web Advocacy (OWA) An international coalition of software developers and technology experts advocating for an open, competitive and interoperable web.
- European Digital Rights (EDRi) Europe's largest network of digital rights organisations, bringing together over 50 organisations to defend fundamental rights and freedoms in the digital age.
- Norwegian Consumer Council One of the leading national consumer rights organisation internationally recognised for its groundbreaking work on digital consumer protection. Behind the viral video A Day in the Life of an Enshittificator.
- ARTICLE 19 An international human rights organisation dedicated to defending freedom of expression and access to information worldwide.
- Free Software Foundation Europe (FSFE) The Free Software Foundation Europe is a charity that empowers users to control technology. The FSFE helps individuals and organisations to understand how Free Software contributes to freedom, transparency, and self-determination.
- European Citizen Action Service (ECAS) An international, Brussels-based non-profit organisation whose mission is to empower citizens in order to create a more inclusive and stronger European Union by promoting and defending citizens’ rights.
- Bits of Freedom A Dutch non-profit advocacy group focused on digital rights such as the right to privacy and communication.
- Digitalcourage A German civil rights organisation with decades of experience defending privacy, individual freedoms and democratic values in the digital age.
- Croatian Alliance of Consumers (UPH) A consumer federation in Croatia established to unite consumer protection groups and advocate for consumer rights.
- Blueprint for Free Speech An international human rights charity dedicated to defending freedom of expression, protecting whistleblowers and strengthening press freedom worldwide.
Publishers and Journalists
- European Publishers Council (EPC) A coalition of Europe's leading media groups committed to defending freedom of expression, editorial independence and media pluralism, safeguarding the economic foundations of independent journalism and ensuring that publishers can compete fairly alongside digital gatekeepers.
- European Federation of Journalists (EFJ) Europe's largest organisation representing journalists, defending the professional rights and editorial independence of over 296,000 journalists through 76 associations and unions across 44 countries.
- European Magazine Media Association (EMMA) The representative voice of more than 15,000 publishing houses producing over 50,000 magazine titles across Europe, advocating for a free, diverse and economically sustainable publishing sector.
- European Newspaper Publishers’ Association (ENPA) Europe's largest representative body of newspaper publishers, bringing together 14 national associations across 14 European countries to defend press freedom and the sustainability of independent journalism.
- News Media Europe (NME) A major voice of Europe's news media industry, representing over 2,700 news brands across print, digital, radio and television.
- Global Forum for Media Development (GFMD) A global network of over 200 member organisations across more than 80 countries, uniting media organisations, journalism support groups and civil society to strengthen independent journalism and defend media freedom worldwide.
- Association of European Journalists in Belgium (AEJ Belgium) An independent association bringing together journalists and EU correspondents to defend press freedom, democratic transparency and independent journalism.
Competition and Economic Policy
- Open Markets Institute Europe A competition policy think tank advancing antitrust enforcement, tackling concentrated corporate power and promoting open, competitive markets across Europe.
- Balanced Economy Project An international non-profit organisation tackling excessive corporate power and working towards a fairer, more democratic economy.
- Enforce (Irish Council for Civil Liberties) A specialist initiative advancing digital rights through strategic litigation, regulatory enforcement and technical expertise.
- Innovate Europe Foundation An independent think tank bringing together European technology and business leaders to strengthen Europe's technological sovereignty, innovation and competitiveness.
- Rebalance Now A German non-profit organisation advancing competition policy and tackling monopoly power through research, advocacy and regulatory engagement.
- iconomy A Berlin-based consultancy and venture builder supporting European technology startups and fair competition in digital markets.
Industry Associations, Startups and Investors
- European DIGITAL SME Alliance Europe's largest network of digital SMEs, championing innovation, competitiveness and digital entrepreneurship.
- Y Combinator A world-leading startup accelerator that has helped launch and scale some of the world's most successful technology companies, including Stripe, Airbnb, DoorDash, Reddit and Dropbox.
- France Digitale A leading European startup association, advancing entrepreneurship, investment and technological innovation.
- German Startup Association A leading voice for Germany's startup ecosystem, advocating for innovation and entrepreneurship.
- Coalition for App Fairness A global coalition advocating for fair competition, consumer choice and open digital marketplaces.
- Movement for an Open Web (MOW) A business coalition that has played a role in challenging Google's dominance of digital advertising through competition complaints and regulatory action.
- European Games Developer Federation A leading representative of Europe's video game development industry, promoting innovation and competitiveness.
- Little Tech Association An organisation supporting technology startups, entrepreneurship and fair competition in digital markets.
Browsers
- Mozilla The non-profit-backed organisation behind Firefox, working to build an open, accessible and competitive internet.
- Vivaldi An independent European browser developer with an emphasis on user choice, privacy and customisation.
- Brave A privacy-focused browser and search engine developer.
- The Tor Project A non-profit organisation developing technologies that protect online anonymity, privacy and freedom from censorship.
Search Engines
- Ecosia A search engine that dedicates its profits to climate action, reforestation and environmental restoration worldwide.
- Qwant A European search engine with a focus on user privacy and European digital sovereignty.
- Kagi An independent, subscription-based search engine offering high-quality, advertisement-free search.
Software, E-Commerce and Online Services
- Shopify One of the world's leading commerce platforms, powering millions of businesses globally and powering hundreds of thousands of online stores across Europe.
- Fastmail A leading independent email provider delivering privacy-focused email, calendar and productivity services to individuals and businesses worldwide.
- Cryptee An Estonian technology company providing end-to-end encrypted document editing, photo management and cloud storage.
- Pelican Party Studios An independent Dutch video game studio developing innovative multiplayer browser games, including the popular Narrow.one.
- Soundslice An innovative web-based music education platform combining interactive sheet music, audio and video to transform how musicians learn, practise and teach music.
- Krellian A technology company developing open standards and web-based technologies to make buildings smarter, safer and more sustainable.
Web Development, Infrastructure and Developer Communities
- Vercel A leading cloud infrastructure and web development platform behind Next.js, powering hundreds of thousands of websites across Europe and millions worldwide.
- Clearleft A digital design consultancy with a longstanding contribution to web standards, accessibility and user experience.
- QuirksMode.org A resource for browser compatibility research, web standards and cross-browser development.
- London Web Standards An established web developer community promoting open web standards and organising the renowned State of the Browser conference.
- RUMvision An innovative web performance monitoring platform helping businesses deliver faster and more accessible web experiences.
- meet.js A Polish JavaScript developer community advancing technical knowledge, collaboration and innovation through conferences and meetups.
See the Full List Of Signatures
The Commission Must Now Act
We welcome the Commission's recognition that browser competition is a priority under the DMA. However in the more than two and a half years after Apple's obligations came into effect, not a single browser vendor has been able to upgrade its existing EU users to its own browser engine.
The extraordinary breadth of this coalition demonstrates both the importance of the web as a competitive platform and the scale of the opportunity being denied to European consumers and businesses.
Apple cannot be allowed to indefinitely frustrate the objectives of the DMA through successive rounds of ineffective compliance. The Commission must now move beyond dialogue, commence formal enforcement proceedings and compel Apple to remove the remaining barriers to effective browser and web app competition.
The web has waited long enough.
A free and competitive web on iOS
Europe is rallying.
Add your voice.
Tell the European Commission: it’s time to enforce the DMA and let the web compete.
Sign the letter
Unlock the Web on Mobile

====================================================================================================

- European Publishers Council (EPC) A coalition of Europe's leading media groups committed to defending freedom of expression, editorial independence and media pluralism, safeguarding the economic foundations of independent journalism and ensuring that publishers can compete fairly alongside digital gatekeepers.
- European Federation of Journalists (EFJ) Europe's largest organisation representing journalists, defending the professional rights and editorial independence of over 296,000 journalists through 76 associations and unions across 44 countries.
- European Magazine Media Association (EMMA) The representative voice of more than 15,000 publishing houses producing over 50,000 magazine titles across Europe, advocating for a free, diverse and economically sustainable publishing sector.
- European Newspaper Publishers’ Association (ENPA) Europe's largest representative body of newspaper publishers, bringing together 14 national associations across 14 European countries to defend press freedom and the sustainability of independent journalism.
- News Media Europe (NME) A major voice of Europe's news media industry, representing over 2,700 news brands across print, digital, radio and television.
- Global Forum for Media Development (GFMD) A global network of over 200 member organisations across more than 80 countries, uniting media organisations, journalism support groups and civil society to strengthen independent journalism and defend media freedom worldwide.
- Association of European Journalists in Belgium (AEJ Belgium) An independent association bringing together journalists and EU correspondents to defend press freedom, democratic transparency and independent journalism.
Competition and Economic Policy
- Open Markets Institute Europe A competition policy think tank advancing antitrust enforcement, tackling concentrated corporate power and promoting open, competitive markets across Europe.
- Balanced Economy Project An international non-profit organisation tackling excessive corporate power and working towards a fairer, more democratic economy.
- Enforce (Irish Council for Civil Liberties) A specialist initiative advancing digital rights through strategic litigation, regulatory enforcement and technical expertise.
- Innovate Europe Foundation An independent think tank bringing together European technology and business leaders to strengthen Europe's technological sovereignty, innovation and competitiveness.
