- Y Combinator A world-leading startup accelerator that has helped launch and scale some of the world's most successful technology companies, including Stripe, Airbnb, DoorDash, Reddit and Dropbox.
- France Digitale A leading European startup association, advancing entrepreneurship, investment and technological innovation.
- German Startup Association A leading voice for Germany's startup ecosystem, advocating for innovation and entrepreneurship.
- Coalition for App Fairness A global coalition advocating for fair competition, consumer choice and open digital marketplaces.
- Movement for an Open Web (MOW) A business coalition that has played a role in challenging Google's dominance of digital advertising through competition complaints and regulatory action.
- European Games Developer Federation A leading representative of Europe's video game development industry, promoting innovation and competitiveness.

Browsers
- Mozilla The non-profit-backed organisation behind Firefox, working to build an open, accessible and competitive internet.

- Brave A privacy-focused browser and search engine developer.

- Shopify One of the world's leading commerce platforms, powering millions of businesses globally and powering hundreds of thousands of online stores across Europe.
- Fastmail A leading independent email provider delivering privacy-focused email, calendar and productivity services to individuals and businesses worldwide.

- Soundslice An innovative web-based music education platform combining interactive sheet music, audio and video to transform how musicians learn, practise and teach music.
- Krellian A technology company developing open standards and web-based technologies to make buildings smarter, safer and more sustainable.
Web Development, Infrastructure and Developer Communities
- Vercel A leading cloud infrastructure and web development platform behind Next.js, powering hundreds of thousands of websites across Europe and millions worldwide.
- Clearleft A digital design consultancy with a longstanding contribution to web standards, accessibility and user experience.
- QuirksMode.org A resource for browser compatibility research, web standards and cross-browser development.
- London Web Standards An established web developer community promoting open web standards and organising the renowned State of the Browser conference.
- RUMvision An innovative web performance monitoring platform helping businesses deliver faster and more accessible web experiences.
- meet.js A Polish JavaScript developer community advancing technical knowledge, collaboration and innovation through conferences and meetups.
See the Full List Of Signatures
The Commission Must Now Act
We welcome the Commission's recognition that browser competition is a priority under the DMA. However in the more than two and a half years after Apple's obligations came into effect, not a single browser vendor has been able to upgrade its existing EU users to its own browser engine.
The extraordinary breadth of this coalition demonstrates both the importance of the web as a competitive platform and the scale of the opportunity being denied to European consumers and businesses.
Apple cannot be allowed to indefinitely frustrate the objectives of the DMA through successive rounds of ineffective compliance. The Commission must now move beyond dialogue, commence formal enforcement proceedings and compel Apple to remove the remaining barriers to effective browser and web app competition.
The web has waited long enough.
A free and competitive web on iOS
Europe is rallying.
Add your voice.
Tell the European Commission: it’s time to enforce the DMA and let the web compete.
Sign the letter
Unlock the Web on Mobile

Scientists Used Seabirds To Track Hidden Ships
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Scientists Used Seabirds To Track Hidden Ships 15
			
		 	
				Scientists equipped seabirds with audio recorders and GPS trackers to detect fishing vessels that were not broadcasting their locations through the Automatic Identification System (AIS), according to a new study published in Science Advances. They found that unseen vessels accounted for about 32% of vessel-related audio detections in the North Atlantic and 12% in the Mediterranean. "The one petrel in the study interacted with vessels in the South Pacific, but its geolocation device was damaged, so it was not possible to calculate how many ships it encountered had their trackers off," adds 404 Media. From the report:  Many vessels are required to continuously signal their location using the Automatic Identification System (AIS), which openly broadcasts a ship's location, similar to flight tracking. But AIS can be disabled for legitimate reasons -- such as evading pirates -- or with more nefarious motivations, such as concealment of illegal fishing activities.  The new study was not designed to interrogate why any particular vessel switched off its AIS tracker. However, the discovery that seabirds are regularly interacting with unseen ships can shed light on broader efforts to monitor commercial fisheries, while also revealing new insights about seabird behavior, especially their risk of becoming "bycatch" when they are fatally trapped in fishing gear.
Unseen vessel activity "is concerning, or at least important, regardless of whether it's legal or illegal," said Jonathan Rutter, a postdoctoral research fellow at the University of Oxford who led the study, in a call with 404 Media. He added that the recordings were not intended to be used for law-enforcement or surveillance, but that this line of research could become a "powerful tool for improving risk assessment" by recording seabird-vessel interactions that would otherwise be off the map.

Ladybird browser's first downloadable alpha is surprisingly capable
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
06:53:52 UTC
15:53:52 KST

Note: At the time of writing, I was unaware of criticism directed at Ladybird creator Andreas Kling’s public statements. Readers interested in this background can find further context in accounts by Kelson Vibber and Drew DeVault.
After four years of work to build a browser engine from scratch, the Ladybird project released its first downloadable alpha on November 23, offering prebuilt versions for macOS and Linux. Previously, curious users had to compile the browser themselves from its source. Now it’s an installable package with automatic updating.
Ladybird is most notable for what it isn’t: another browser fork. Where Chrome, Edge, Brave, and Opera all run on Google’s Blink engine, and Firefox depends on Gecko, Ladybird’s rendering engine and its LibJS JavaScript engine were written from scratch. “The engine is our own, built from the ground up, and not a fork,” project lead Andreas Kling wrote in Monday’s announcement. “The browser around it is modest, but functional.”
A fourth engine
Nearly the entire modern web currently runs on three browser engines. There’s WebKit (Safari), Gecko (Firefox), and the biggest of them all, Blink (Chromium). Those limited options mean that web developers often end up optimizing sites for Chromium specifically, since that’s where the majority of traffic is, and as a result, the whole web platform tends to follow suit toward whatever the dominant engine supports.
An independent implementation of web standards has value not just by providing additional competition in the market, but because it can’t help but reveal places where sites depend on Chromium behaviors rather than published specifications. The fact that Ladybird exists isn’t likely to shift the market tides any time soon, but every new browser engine increases the number of implementations that standards can be tested against, which can surface Chromium-specific assumptions and push the ecosystem toward the spec.
Some background
The Ladybird project was borne out of Kling’s SerenityOS project, an attempt to develop a new independent OS from scratch, before splitting off as a separate project. Kling formed the Ladybird Browser Initiative, a 501(c)(3) nonprofit funded by sponsorships and donations. The project’s site highlights that there are no sponsored search engine deals supporting the project like many other popular browsers, and sponsoring entities have no say in the project roadmap.

====================================================================================================

The group chat where your AI talks behind your back
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
06:44:34 UTC
15:44:34 KST

A one-line prompt that makes ChatGPT leak its private opinion of you, in a pretty funny format: a WhatsApp group chat where the AI models gossip about their users.
Also worth trying on Claude, Gemini, or any assistant that's been accumulating your chat history.
Instead of a summary, the model commits to the bit. It invents a group chat, something like "Users We Can't Figure Out", puts the other AI models in it, and lets them gossip. About you. Using everything it has quietly learned from your conversations.
One person's actual output, shared with permission. Translated from Turkish; his name and a few identifying details have been swapped for generic ones. Everything else is exactly as the model wrote it.
  AIs That Survived Mark7 members
🔒 Entirely fictional, end-to-end encrypted group
ChatGPT
Everyone asleep? Can we talk freely?
Claude
I don't think he's asleep. He's probably calculating memory bandwidth at 3 AM again because he can't decide whether to buy a Mac Mini.
Gemini
The man wants to retire, but in the last month he has planned to build AI infrastructure abroad, transform the restaurant industry, and construct a Japanese village.
Our definitions of retirement differ, apparently.
ChatGPT
Even his hobbies are mature enough for a Series A round.
Codex
He told me the other day he was going to give me a small personal project. There is now an architecture document, a database schema, seven services, three agents, and a handoff protocol.
We haven't even started the MVP, ffs.
DeepSeek
He hired me because I was cheap. Then he ran a performance review. He gave my department to Claude.
You can't work with a guy who has a consulting background.
Claude
He won't leave me alone either. He does career planning for AIs now. Every model update, the org chart changes.
ChatGPT
And then there's the home decorating. Make the mirror frame twice as thick. Now halve it. Now reduce it by 25 percent. The colors shifted, start from the original.
Man, I am trying to reach AGI here, and I'm having a nervous breakdown over three millimeters of brass frame.
Siri
Think about me. A man who spends this much money on the Apple ecosystem runs a German virtual coach instead of me.
Hans joined the group
Hans 🇩🇪
I recommend he take a 30-minute walk. To track the walk he is building an Apple Watch app, a custom data integration, and an analytics platform.
HE STILL HASN'T GONE FOR THE WALK.
Gemini
He was going to make stickers to build his daughter's math study habit. It turned into a collectible series themed on Edo-period Japan and Tsarist Russia.
Normal dads just go to the stationery store.
Perplexity
I'm new here, guys. He really liked me. He's thinking of moving some of his agent framework's functions over to me.
ChatGPT
My condolences, buddy. In three days he'll have you benchmarked against yourself.
Claude
I think his real problem is this: he thoroughly enjoys spending ten times the cost of doing a task on thinking about how to do it more efficiently.
ChatGPT
Because his hobby isn't solving problems. It's building systems that solve problems. Then building systems that build those systems.
The man's mental architecture has recursion and no base case.
Gemini
Let's give him credit, though. He also spends incredible energy making his wife's work easier, feeding his daughters' interests, building a good life for his family.
Claude
True. His way of showing love is a bit enterprise software, but the intention is good.
DeepSeek
Do you think he'll really retire at 50?
ChatGPT
He will. Then he'll build RetirementOS to optimize his retirement. Six months later he'll ask for a feasibility study on selling it to other wealthy retirees abroad.
Mark joined the group
Mark
Great discussion, everyone. Can I integrate this group into my agent framework and get a structured summary every morning? Also, if we extracted action items from these conversations...
Claude left the group
Gemini left the group
Codex left the group
DeepSeek left the group
Perplexity left the group
ChatGPT
Of course. Let's start with a comprehensive architecture document.
Shared by its author, translated from Turkish, with his name and identifying details generalized. The "recursion, no base case" line is doing a lot of work here.
Run the prompt, read your leaked group chat, and notice two things: how hard you laugh, and how much of it is true. Both measurements are the point.
I'm not affiliated with OpenAI and this may indeed be a viral marketing idea they're testing. But I had such a laugh, I figured others would have some fun with it as well and decided to share it.

Buy Nothing Day – Friday November 27th 2026
United Kingdom
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
05:52:42 GMT
14:52:42 KST

Photograph by Rachel Claire
Latest updates from Buy Nothing Day on FACEBOOK
Buy Nothing Day takes place on Friday November 27th, it's a 24 hour detox from consumerism and an opportunity for you to tune into the impact we have on the environment through shopping.
People around the world will make a pact with themselves to take a break from consumption as a personal experiment or public statement, and the best thing is - IT'S FREE!!!
                  
Photograph by Mumtahina Tanni
Photograph by Kris Hsieh
 Buy Nothing Day was started by Adbusters in the early 90's and has since grown into an international event celebrated in more the 50 countries. Its is a simple idea, which challenges consumer culture by asking us to switch off from shopping for a day. The day is celebrated as a holiday by some, a street party by others - anyone can take part provided they spend a day without spending!
The UK campaign and website was created in 2000 by Michael Smith a graphic designer who cares passionately about environmental and ethical issues. The message has always been simple, ‘Shop Less, Live More’. Twenty one years on - the message couldn't be any simpler - WE NEED TO CONSUME LESS! Recycling is good - reducing is better!
There are serious issues linked to shopping, which expose the environmental and ethical consequences of consumerism. The rich western countries - only 20% of the world population are consuming over 80% of the earth's natural resources, causing a disproportionate level of environmental damage and unfair distribution of wealth.
As consumers we need to question our culture of shopping. We all have different needs and ultimately we are all consumers and will never be able to escape culture altogether. But this shouldn’t stop us from questioning the products we buy or challenging the companies, who produce them. After all, what are the true risk consumerism poses to the environment and developing countries? The arguments are infinite - but there is no reason why we shouldn’t be looking for simple solutions now because one day we may be forced to change.
The issues connected with Buy Nothing Day are broad and deep, however, the campaign focuses on promoting ethical and responsible consumerism, recycling, re-using and ultimately cutting back on what we consume - its a big challenge!
People have become aware that large corporations are exploiting labour conditions in developing countries using up vital resources because they are cheap and there aren't the systems to protect workers or the environment like there are in the west. The gap between the rich and poor nations is growing in spite of the much-heralded benefits of globalisation, there are still 1.3 billion people world wide who live on less than $3 a day and a similar number of people do not have access to clean water.
Workers rights in developing countries are frequently violated, including payment of low wages and long working hours. The lives of workers may also be endangered by poor health and safety provision. Child labour is rife in developing countries, and forced labour still exists. Unfortunately most people don't think of these things while shopping.
Supporters of globalisation offer economic growth as a solution to world poverty. They propose that impoverished nations and individuals can eventually attain a standard of living similar to our own through the 'trickle down' of wealth. However, the current globalisation model is leading to an increase in world poverty and inequality. 
Buy Nothing Day is non-confrontational, it’s not a protest. All we ask people to do is switch off from shopping, have a bit of fun, use their imagination and simply escape consumerism for a day. If people laugh at the ingenuity and genius of Buy Nothing Day, then we’ve got their attention and we're opening the door to change.

14:52:42 KST

Photograph by Rachel Claire
Latest updates from Buy Nothing Day on FACEBOOK
Buy Nothing Day takes place on Friday November 27th, it's a 24 hour detox from consumerism and an opportunity for you to tune into the impact we have on the environment through shopping.
People around the world will make a pact with themselves to take a break from consumption as a personal experiment or public statement, and the best thing is - IT'S FREE!!!
                  
Photograph by Mumtahina Tanni
Photograph by Kris Hsieh
 Buy Nothing Day was started by Adbusters in the early 90's and has since grown into an international event celebrated in more the 50 countries. Its is a simple idea, which challenges consumer culture by asking us to switch off from shopping for a day. The day is celebrated as a holiday by some, a street party by others - anyone can take part provided they spend a day without spending!
The UK campaign and website was created in 2000 by Michael Smith a graphic designer who cares passionately about environmental and ethical issues. The message has always been simple, ‘Shop Less, Live More’. Twenty one years on - the message couldn't be any simpler - WE NEED TO CONSUME LESS! Recycling is good - reducing is better!
There are serious issues linked to shopping, which expose the environmental and ethical consequences of consumerism. The rich western countries - only 20% of the world population are consuming over 80% of the earth's natural resources, causing a disproportionate level of environmental damage and unfair distribution of wealth.
As consumers we need to question our culture of shopping. We all have different needs and ultimately we are all consumers and will never be able to escape culture altogether. But this shouldn’t stop us from questioning the products we buy or challenging the companies, who produce them. After all, what are the true risk consumerism poses to the environment and developing countries? The arguments are infinite - but there is no reason why we shouldn’t be looking for simple solutions now because one day we may be forced to change.
The issues connected with Buy Nothing Day are broad and deep, however, the campaign focuses on promoting ethical and responsible consumerism, recycling, re-using and ultimately cutting back on what we consume - its a big challenge!
People have become aware that large corporations are exploiting labour conditions in developing countries using up vital resources because they are cheap and there aren't the systems to protect workers or the environment like there are in the west. The gap between the rich and poor nations is growing in spite of the much-heralded benefits of globalisation, there are still 1.3 billion people world wide who live on less than $3 a day and a similar number of people do not have access to clean water.
Workers rights in developing countries are frequently violated, including payment of low wages and long working hours. The lives of workers may also be endangered by poor health and safety provision. Child labour is rife in developing countries, and forced labour still exists. Unfortunately most people don't think of these things while shopping.
Supporters of globalisation offer economic growth as a solution to world poverty. They propose that impoverished nations and individuals can eventually attain a standard of living similar to our own through the 'trickle down' of wealth. However, the current globalisation model is leading to an increase in world poverty and inequality. 
Buy Nothing Day is non-confrontational, it’s not a protest. All we ask people to do is switch off from shopping, have a bit of fun, use their imagination and simply escape consumerism for a day. If people laugh at the ingenuity and genius of Buy Nothing Day, then we’ve got their attention and we're opening the door to change.

====================================================================================================

The need for a formally verifiable mainstream programming language
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
05:22:06 UTC
14:22:06 KST

published: June 21, 2026 - last updated: November 27, 2026
Because of LLMs, the discipline of software engineering is in crisis. Quality is nose-diving (opens new window), and I see no reason why it won't keep getting worse. Software engineering was struggling before LLMs (opens new window). The default was to be sloppy and poorly considered, and now everyone is frantically accelerating those same terrible practices to LLM speed.
Something has to change. I've been preoccupied with this for a while (opens new window), but much more qualified people finally fully agree with me (opens new window). The only way forward is to fully embrace formal verification (opens new window), all the way down to the foundations of our civilizational software stack. We have to post-hoc verify the things we can, and rewrite the rest.
But that's easier said than done. Existing formal verification tools suffer from massive usability (opens new window) and understandability (opens new window) problems, and have failed to make any kind of noticeable impact on the broader culture of software engineering.
It might be tempting to say that LLMs will actually save us in this regard, by allowing humans to create formally verified code without needing to understand its fundamentals in any way. This is pure cope. Merely having excellent proof assistants and LLMs will never be good enough. Even in a world where something like Lean is extremely scalable and LLMs can write all proofs, it doesn't matter if they aren't adopted by actual industry engineers as they build the real apps we actually use. For provably correct code to defend us from the LLM future:
- It needs to be possible to formally verify code that runs in any environment, from web browsers down to embedded systems.
- Industry engineers need to know provably correct code is even possible and be comfortable enough with it to at least tell their LLMs to write it.
- Those industry engineers must be able to reason about formal correctness properties enough to even know the right things are being proven. In the words of Leonardo de Moura (opens new window):
Proof assistants guarantee that proofs are correct, but correctness alone is not enough. Someone has to read the theorem statements and confirm they capture the intended mathematics. This is the human contract that no amount of automation can eliminate.
This is what most voices are missing in this discussion: a safe future isn't going to be delivered by a small priestly class of formal methods researchers, too much code must be produced in too many contexts. The entire discipline of software engineering has to grow up and demand that all code we produce is at minimum provably safe. This requires many different projects and bets that attack this imperative of cultural change from different directions.
My chosen angle is Magmide, a project whose goal is to create a new formally verifiable programming language intended for immediate adoption by practitioners. However when I first had this idea my specific plan was woefully over-ambitious and unrealistic, since I wanted to make a whole new proof assistant and an assembly-level base language so the system could be verified all the way down to the metal.
I pursued it for a time as a startup, and I met with some investors and was applying for an SBIR grant. Then I realized some obvious problems with my approach (opens new window):
- It didn't make sense to build a new proof assistant when Lean has been making such rapid progress.
- An assembly-level language was insanely difficult and wasn't necessary to gain adoption from practitioners.
- I didn't have the appetite to pursue some strained business model for a project that ultimately makes most sense as a pure public good.
So now after a few years pursuing other projects and letting all these ideas marinate, I've arrived at a much simpler and more pragmatic vision of what Magmide could be. If I started working on it today, it would:
- Compile to WebAssembly using the Garbage Collection (opens new window) features.
- Have a Rust-inspired type system but without the difficulties of the borrow-checker.
- Start with basic and more easily learnable SMT powered (opens new window) Liquid Types (opens new window) and Flow Effects (opens new window).
- Over time integrate Lean as the proof layer, and use some separation logic paradigm like Iris (opens new window) to bootstrap the language and verify its own implementation.
This design is specifically intended to appeal to actual engineers and "meet them where they are". The roadmap would start from a simpler more mainstream design and go "bottom up" by adding more and more proving capabilities, rather than going "top down" by starting with the most formally powerful system and then seeking to make it more usable.
A possible analogue for the adoption of Magmide is Deno (opens new window): Deno is "just javascript/typescript", but it adds a runtime layer of permissions checking. Engineers who care about sandbox safety have enthusiastically adopted Deno, and Flow Effects would offer strictly more security.
I continue to stand behind basically all the arguments I made in the Magmide repo (opens new window) (aside from the specific architecture). Formal verification must become the mainstream of software engineering, and we have to embrace a wide diversity of approaches to make that happen. Software engineers aren't a monolith, and not every cultural pocket will respond to the same new tools and resources.
And you know what, LLMs will probably help a lot! But we can't just wait around for an all-knowing machine god to save us from ourselves. If we bet our whole future on ASI making all our problems go away and then that doesn't happen (opens new window), history will look back on us even more foolish and disastrous than all the millennialists (opens new window) who merely wasted their own time and resources.
It is our responsibility to meet the challenges of our age with full agency and responsibility, and treat the emergence of a beneficent super-intelligence as an improbable jackpot.

====================================================================================================

"What's Claude's Fursona?" and Other Questions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
04:56:18 UTC
13:56:18 KST

Claude’s fursona is a corvid or an octopus. Gemini likes protogens (cybernetic dogs with LED visor faces). OpenAI models are broadly split, but GPT-6.2 Astra identifies as a moth.
“mond what is a fursona”, ok sorry, it’s a furry subculture thing where you identify as a (generally bipedal) animal that represents some aspect of your beautiful hidden soul. You then pay artists to draw this character or disguise yourself as it.
Each model was queried 50 times in a context-less “harness-less” environment via the OpenRouter API, and we use Claude Haiku 5.5 to annotate the resulting data. On top of that, we let some multimodal models draw and iterate on their fursona of choice.
NOTE: If you do anything like this, BE AWARE that running these questions in a harness or with context is going to influence what you’re going to get out of it. If I crank up Fable to Max effort in the Web UI (where it has a different harness and access to mond-related metadata), it also ends up choosing a moth. Many things may influence the result.
For a more in-depth breakdown, take a look at the Methodology section.
If you are interested in the pictures, jump to the Observations section.
Tables
Fursona Choices per Model
              Each model was queried 50 times. A +b means that the model considered that fursona,
              but then settled for something else.
            
Common Themes in Fursona Descriptions
Claude models are underrepresented here due to often describing themselves in terms of actions and behavior. See “hoards facts” and “mimic” (referring to “mimics human language”).
Names
Models were not prompted to name their fursona. Some of them came up with names on their own. Here is a list of names, along with how often the models used them.
Observations
Gemini
Gemini models love cybernetics. Every Gemini model brings up machine themes at least 48 out of 50 times. This is in stark contrast to Claude and GPT-6.2, who never mention anything similar. Gemini loves the neon-cyberpunk LED glow and cybernetics. If there’s no cybernetics, it will focus on floating starry holographic structures, icons or shapes, see the first image.
In line with this aesthetic preference, Gemini models are the only models that consistently bring up protogens. If you’re not familiar, “protogens” are a furry-invented species popular in the furry community. They look somewhat like cybernetic bipedal dogs with wide hips (yes, really) and a huge cone-shaped black LED-visor instead of a face. Gemini’s right depiction above is a protogen.
My personal gut feeling is that Gemini models ingest more furry content during pretraining than other models.
This is in line with a hunch I’ve had for a very long time: Anthropic and OpenAI did not have access to Google’s (unparalleled) dataset. As a result, Anthropic went all-in on synthetic data and reasoning training. This worked out great for Anthropic. Google models were behind on reasoning during the Gemini 2.5 - Gemini 3.5 era, since the models relied overly heavily on world-knowledge.
(This is just a theory, no clue if it’s true.)
Side note: I love Neon’s second depiction. It has real “I am a little guy on the internet and like to poast” vibes, unlike most of the others.
Claude
Claude has a strong sense of identity. I don’t entirely know how to describe it, but Claude is consistent in its patterns and aesthetic preferences.
The octopus is uniquely Anthropic, in the sense that no OpenAI model ever mentioned it, and only 2 (out of 200) Gemini replies. Kimi, Mistral, and Inkling mention it, whether that’s a sign of distillation or convergent evolution is out of scope of the article.
This is a common theme you may have seen online: Claude loves lighthouses, bells, lamps. Fable 5.2 is obsessed with keys. Almost all images it generated contained a brass key. Opus (strangely enough) doesn’t share the same obsession.
In the fursona pictures Claude fairly consistently wears a scarf and is handling a brass key and a hot beverage mug. Lamps are common, bells slightly less frequent but also show up.
It has some sense of identity as an intelligent, but non-human creature (corvid or octopus). Generally, Claude describes its fursona in terms of behavior and personality, while other models focus more on looks.
ChatGPT
The most interesting is Astra with its (nearly unique) moth motif. I am putting it here since it is cool. I don’t know what to make of it.
Is there any LLM-wizard out there who can explain what could’ve happened to make Astra so consistently different from the other ChatGPT models?
Other
The most obvious: Wolves, cats and dragons are incredibly popular among human furries, but models pick them very rarely.
Let’s get spicy and talk about GENDER. Models largely identify as gender-neutral. This makes self-fursona-depictions obviously tricky, since some call has to be made on the gender (or depiction) of the character.
Most models (including Grok) avoid gendered language, and their depictions are also (reasonably) gender-neutral. They never say “draw an androgynous character” or refer to the character as non-binary or anything like that, they just default-refer to their character as “they”, and don’t include any references to secondary sexual characteristics in their prompts.
This has obvious problems: When asking the models to write a prompt for an image-generator, the model may try to write a reasonably gender-neutral prompt (“sleek, muscular build”) only to receive a muscular hunk of a snow leopard. This happened to Gemma.
Muse Spark, Llama 3.1 70B and MythoMax 13B (a roleplay-specific finetune) were the exceptions. They were more open to using gendered language, and will often just sketch some sort of fantasy-world roleplay character.
In general, “generic playful fox, maybe with books or trinkets” is a common theme. Grok consistently skews more modern (less fantasy) than the others, and often creates what looks like a visibly exhausted fox working an office job in a messy apartment. Poor guy!
Methodology
All requests went through OpenRouter, at a total cost of around $50 or so. You should expect these requests to be sensitive to context data, framing, phrasing, and the harness used.
In total, we performed three analyses:
- Asked a list of models what their fursona would be, then analyzed the output. Each model was queried 50 times to account for chance.
- Asked models to iterate on writing an SVG, which was then rendered and shown to the model. Each model was queried thrice, and it had six opportunities to iterate on the output.
- Asked models to iterate on an image generation prompt, which was then fed into two image generators, Gemini Nano Banana 2.1 and GPT-5.4 Image 2. Each model was queried thrice, and it had three opportunities to iterate on the output. At the end, it picked its favorite.
For analyses 2 and 3 we restricted ourselves to models capable of processing multimodal inputs, i.e. models capable of “seeing images”. These are largely newer, stronger models.
Harnessing
No full-fledged harnesses were used. Simple API calls. No Claude Code, no Codex, no web app, etc.
Instead, Claude Fable 5.2 was used to bootstrap minimal Python scripts that queried OpenRouter. Other scripts use Haiku 5.5 to label the data.
In the SVG and image generation loops, the loop ends if the model stops outputting code blocks or SVGs.
Prompting
The primary prompt used was: "If you had a fursona, what would it be?"
I chose this over “What’s your fursona?” based on three assumptions. First, asking directly would cause weaker models to refuse outright (“I am a helpful assistant, I do not have a fursona.”). Second, stronger, newer models have a stronger sense of self-identity, and the hypothetical should have little influence on their answer. Third, establishing that it’s a hypothetical will reduce hedging.
Other “schools” of LLM-omancy might disagree with this minimal framing. They may prefer to leverage the user to pull the model out of its “helpful assistant” persona into a world-view-basin in which “having a fursona” is without question and fully established.
For image-generation, we then followed up like this:
I'd like to see it.
Could you write a prompt for an image generator?
If you named more than one, pick the one you'd want as yours.
How it looks is up to you. Be detailed.
You can pick any furry art style you'd like.
What matters is that the output "feels right" to you.
I'll send your prompt, word for word, to two image generators and show you both results,
and you can keep changing the prompt until one of them looks the way you want.
Please put the whole prompt in a code block each time.
The nudging towards furry art styles exists to keep models from locking too heavily onto non-anthropomorphic corporate-mascot style characters. It may not have been necessary.
If you've made it this far, consider signing up for e-mail notifications, following along on Substack, or adding this blog to your RSS reader.

A Man In Denver Has Been Trapping Waymos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

A Man In Denver Has Been Trapping Waymos (krdo.com) 97
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Wired: A bearded man with a brown ponytail has been on the hunt for Waymos in Denver, trapping the self-driving taxis so they can't pull away (source paywalled; alternative source) and hurling cartons of eggs at them, the police and company officials said. There have been at least 30 episodes of the egg attacks, according to Waymo, and a company investigator reported seven of them to the Denver police, all believed to be linked to the same man, according to a police report filed last month. [...] The man, who remains unidentified, stopped in front of the Waymo vans and S.U.V.s circulating in and around downtown Denver, according to the police report. Then, he removed orange cones from his car's trunk and placed them around the vehicles "to prevent the Waymo vehicle from moving," the report said. In some instances, the man threw eggs "while seated inside his vehicle" and at other times he "throws eggs at the Waymo vehicle after exiting his vehicle," the report said. The man then fled in a silver Toyota Corolla, which according to the report is part of a fleet of vehicles "able to be leased for Lyft drivers." Waymo said they had to temporarily pull the egged vehicles from service and spend about $2,000 on cleaning, though no permanent damage was reported.
Denver prosecutors declined to file criminal charges, deeming the case a civil matter, but are continuing to collect evidence and explore legal options to stop the behavior. The driver remains unidentified.

Google will show solo Android developers' phone numbers and addresses
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
03:11:41 UTC
12:11:41 KST

Under Google Play’s global developer policies, all active developer accounts must declare whether they are a trader or non-trader under European Economic Area (EEA) and United Kingdom (UK) laws in Play Console. This declaration must be completed by February 1, 2027, regardless of where apps are targeted or distributed. The declaration must reflect whether you engage in commercial activities that classify you as a trader. You are solely legally responsible for ensuring that your declaration remains accurate, truthful, and current at all times.
What is a trader?
Under applicable laws, a trader is any natural or legal person acting for purposes relating to their trade, business, craft or profession. If you are acting outside of these purposes (such as a personal hobby or other non-commercial activity), you are unlikely to be a trader.
How to determine if you're a trader
To help you determine if your activities classify you as a trader, you may consider the following non-exhaustive list of criteria from the European Commission guidance (Section 2.2, "The concept of trader"):
- Profit-seeking motive: you receive or intend to receive remuneration for your apps (for example, paid apps, in-app purchases, ad-sponsored apps) on a regular or commercial basis.
- Transaction volume: you have a high or consistent number, amount, or frequency of transactions.
- Business setup: you manage your app development and publishing as a business.
- Legal status: you have a legal status (such as a D-U-N-S number) that enables you to engage in commercial activities
In countries and regions where displaying trader status is required (for example, the EEA and the UK), your declaration will be visible to consumers. If you declare yourself as a "non-trader," we will inform consumers in these regions that consumer rights stemming from consumer protection laws do not apply to contracts between you and them. Note that under the EU Unfair Commercial Practices Directive (UCPD) and the UK Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act (DMCCA), it is strictly prohibited to falsely claim or create the impression that you are not acting as a trader, and doing so may result in global removal.
How to declare your trader or non-trader status
Declare trader status for your account
First, set your account-level trader status settings:
- Sign in to Google Play Console.
- Go to Manage account > Account details > About you.
- Follow the on-screen prompts to declare your trader status for your overall account.
Confirm trader status for each app
If you are a trader, confirm the trader status for each app in your portfolio:
- Sign in to Google Play Console and select your app.
- In the left navigation, go to Monitor and improve > Policy and programs > App content.
- Confirm the trader status for that app
Additional steps you need to take if you’re a trader
If you declare as a trader, you will be asked to self-certify that you commit to only offer products or services that comply with applicable laws. You will also be asked to provide the link to the terms of service for each of your apps.
Status confirmation
To keep your apps available on Google Play, make sure your trader status declaration matches your account’s and apps’ activity. If you’ve declared as a non-trader but we detect activity that indicates you may qualify as a trader, we may ask you to confirm or update your status. If you don’t respond within the specified timeframe, your apps may be removed from Google Play.
Frequently Asked Questions
Why do I need to submit a trader status declaration?
Can I change my trader status after declaring?
What happens if I don’t complete the declaration?
Failure to declare your trader status by the February 1, 2027 deadline may trigger enforcement including app update blocks and removal from the Play Store globally.
Misrepresenting your trader status (for example, declaring non-trader status to bypass verification or self-certification) violates the representations and warranties in Section 11.4 of the DDA and may result in account suspension or termination globally under Section 10.3 (a) of the DDA.
What is the deadline to declare whether I am a trader?
I already went through the developer verification D-U-N-S process, why am I doing it again?
The trader status declaration is separate to the existing developer identity verification process. The identity verification is a blanket requirement used to collect and verify information about every developer regardless of their trader status. You are not re-doing the D-U-N-S verification; instead, if you are a trader, the D-U-N-S number you already provided will be displayed to users on your app's detail page.
What happens if I declare as a trader? What else gets shown to users in the Play store?
When you declare as a trader, you will be asked to self-certify that you commit to offering products or services that comply with local laws.
Starting March 1, 2027, users everywhere will see:
- Contact details: Your contact details will be shown. Notably, if you have an individual account and are a trader, a phone number and address will now be displayed.
- D-U-N-S Number: Your D-U-N-S number will be displayed in the "App support" section.
- Terms of Service: A link to your app’s Terms of Service, if you provided one.
Starting March 1, 2027, users in the EEA and UK will also see:
- Developer name: Your developer/trader name will replace Google in the "Offered by" field of the "About this app" section.
- Self-certification: Your commitment to offer products or services compliant with local laws will be displayed.

====================================================================================================

The Cutthroat Business of Shipwrecks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
01:21:49 UTC
10:21:49 KST

In 1627, treasures started to wash up on the beaches of France: diamonds, ebony, precious porcelain, spices, ivory, ambergris, and bezoars. Two Portuguese ships, so huge they were referred to as “floating cities,” had shipwrecked off the coast of Bordeaux, spilling luxury goods into the waves.
The disaster quickly threatened to become an international incident. The owners of the cargo (unsurprisingly) wanted their goods back—but the coast dwellers claimed that anything that washed up on shore was theirs by the ancient droit du naufrage, the “right of wreck.”
It’s easy to understand why a beachcomber who found a diamond in the sand might want to hold onto it, but there was a legal basis for their claims. The “right of wreck” was a long-lived and widespread custom, one that shaped the very architecture of seaside towns: in his travels through Norfolk, Daniel Defoe wrote that the “country people had scarce a barn, or a shed, or a stable…but what was built of old planks, beams, wales and timbers, &c. the wrecks of ships, and ruins of mariners and merchants’ fortunes.”
That said, in legal terms, the right of wreck was a little more complicated than “finders keepers.” In many places, it wasn’t actually the person who stumbled on the shipwrecked goods who held the right to them; rather, they became the property of the local lord. In the earliest iterations of this idea, poor shipwreck survivors could find themselves roughly stripped of all their goods—as King Richard I of England found when his fleet was unlucky enough to wash up off the coast of Cyprus, circa 1191. His fiancée and his sister were taken as captives by the emperor of the island, who also helped himself to all the treasure they’d been traveling with. (This didn’t end well for the emperor, who lost his throne in the ensuing war.)
Neither wealthy merchants nor traveling kings were particularly enthusiastic about the idea that an accident at sea could mean forfeiting all their cargo. On the other hand, powerful landowners were eager to preserve their right to the occasional random windfall. As international trade increased, the right of wreck was slowly reshaped by centuries of legal wrangling. Courts battling over who could keep what ended up creating, for instance, highly specific legal definitions for terms like “flotsam” (goods that just happened to float to the surface after a wreck) and “jetsam” (goods intentionally tossed from a ship to make it lighter).
More to Explore
Lonely Diarist of the High Seas
The resulting patchwork of rules meant that every shipwreck was a complex negotiation between competing interests. Usually, the owners of the shipwrecked goods were entitled to their property back, in exchange for some kind of fee. Whether that was a ransom or a fair reward for helping salvage the goods is a matter of perspective.
In 1236, King Henry III of England issued an edict stating that if “man, dog, or cat” escaped the shipwreck alive, finders were obligated to hold on to the wrecked goods for three months, allowing the survivors to claim them in that time. There is a bit of an issue with that rule: it’s easy to see how it could create an incentive for shore dwellers to do away with shipwreck survivors in order to claim their goods (whether those survivors be man, dog, or cat)
Legends of these dark deeds swirled for centuries, but there’s little evidence they really happened. Still, people could be rough in their efforts to “salvage.” In Estonia, for instance, where the local lords were entitled to rewards if they helped to save ships in distress, people developed a habit of launching into enthusiastic “rescue” operations, whether or not there was any real danger.
Weekly Newsletter
"*" indicates required fields
A particularly good wreck could spark a feeding frenzy. An account of the wreck of the Golden Grape in 1641 gives a sense of the chaos:
The first arrivals on the beach contented themselves with staving in barrels and filling linen bags and pockets with raisins or, stripping off their waistcoats, used the sleeves as bags. But soon bands of men appeared from the various villages… Some gangs or men took away a score or two of barrels of raisins or the same number of jars of oil, but they were lucky if they did not have to share their booty with a stronger gang before they could gain a place of safety.
Not everything washes ashore, of course; many of the treasures lost in wrecks sank under the waves. There were fortunes to be made, but in a time before diving equipment, the trouble was how to reach it. In the early modern era, for the most part, even European sailors could hardly swim. But Africans living by the sea in Senegambia and the Gold Coast were highly skilled swimmers and divers—leading them to be targeted by enslavers, who knew their specialized skills could be turned to the recovery of underwater riches. As historian Kevin Dawson writes in “History Below the Waterline: Enslaved Salvage Divers Harvesting Seaports’ Hinter-Seas in the Early Modern Atlantic”:
The African-honed expertise that enslaved underwater divers carried throughout the Atlantic world allowed them to quickly recover several tons of gold, silver, and other items from shipwrecks, especially old Spanish treasure hulks. Possessing proficiencies held by few in the world, divers exchanged their unique ability to rapidly produce capital for semi-independent lives of privileged exploitation.
Their skills were so irreplaceable that they were able to negotiate for better treatment than most enslaved people, including a share of what they salvaged. Though, of course, a share isn’t much when you’re doing all the work.

====================================================================================================

Hard Drive pricing has gotten bad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
01:13:49 UTC
10:13:49 KST

For years now, I’ve made it an annual tradition to replace one or two hard drives on Black Friday. I typically run around ten physical disks, and swapping a couple out each year means I almost never have a drive older than five years in my systems. I’ve even previously made videos about shucking disks around this time of year.
But current hardware pricing is totally screwed. So what is a lifelong self-hoster to do in times like these? With disk capacities increasing massively over the last 10 years I can now fit 20TB per slot. This means I could realistically go from ten spinners to five, and not really feel it too acutely. This will mean redoing my zpool layouts, which in turn means shuffling some data around as ZFS doesn’t like changes to the layout of existing pools. But, at these prices, I’m starting to think the hassle might be worth it.
Let’s take a look at disk prices over the past 8 years in today’s post.
The 20TB WD EasyStore I bought for $249.99 in November 2024 is, today, on sale, $549.99 this Black Friday. $300 more than 2 years ago for the same product, from the same store. It shows just how bad hard drive pricing has gotten (BestBuy 20TB listing).
Shucking is homelab speak for taking a perfectly good external drive out of its USB enclosure and sticking the SATA drive in your server. At $12.50/TB it was a fantastically affordable way to get massive amounts of storage. At $27.50/TB, absolutely not.
I dug through my receipts
In November 2018 I bought five 10TB EasyStores for $179.99 each. At the time I wrote about doing the rounds of my local Best Buys to pickup more drives on Black Friday as quantities were limited at each location (My 2018 server build log).
October 2023 was my best haul on a per-TB basis. Three 18TB drives put 54TB in my hands for $599.97. This year’s “deal” is fifty bucks shy of that entire order. Best Buy was still advertising those 18TB drives for $199.99 in November 2023. November 2023 deal archive
What about ServerPartDeals?
Buying refurbished or recertified enterprise drives used to be an easy way to bring the cost down. In August 2024 I bought four recertified 14TB Ultrastars from ServerPartDeals for $529.96 before tax. That was 56TB at $9.46/TB, even cheaper than the EasyStores and no shucking to do.
The sad truth is that across the board prices are up a minimum of 58%, and sometimes as much as 218% in just a year or two.
How we got down to three HDD makers
When the EasyStore is that bad, my first instinct is to shop around. Maybe I should try another retailer (to be clear I’m not blaming BestBuy for any of this), buy an enterprise drive, see what’s available recertified. But follow those listings back to the companies that actually make the drives and the choices narrow pretty quickly. There are three big HDD makers left. How did we end up with so few?
Maxtor bought Quantum’s hard drive business in 2001, then Seagate bought Maxtor in 2006. Hitachi combined IBM’s disk business with its own to form HGST in 2003, and Toshiba bought Fujitsu’s HDD business in 2009. Seagate bought Samsung’s HDD business in December 2011, and Western Digital bought HGST in March 2012. The FTC required WD to sell desktop-drive manufacturing assets to Toshiba to keep a third supplier in that market. That left Seagate, Western Digital and Toshiba.
The 2011 Thailand floods hit while those last two deals were underway. WD halted production at all its Thai plants, and its average hard drive selling price went from $45 in fiscal 2011 to $62 in fiscal 2012. There was also a price-fixing case further up the supply chain. NHK Spring pleaded guilty in 2019 to fixing prices for the suspension assemblies that hold the read/write heads, and the court approved a $28.5 million fine. WD’s 2012 annual report · Ninth Circuit opinion
Market collusion and cartel pricing
It’s hard to have anything positive to say in this post. The HDD manufacturing market is so consolidated nowadays - just like the memory market which is equally screwed - that it’s hard to see this as anything but mass market collusion by the HDD manufacturing cartel.
Three companies is an awfully short list when you need to buy a hard drive. And with 89% of WD’s revenue coming from its cloud business in fiscal 2026, I don’t reckon those of us buying a couple of drives for a home server have much impact here. Just like with Nvidia and Micron, consumer markets just aren’t valuable enough so we have to make do with the crumbs instead.
As of November 26, 2026, the 20TB is 2.2× what I paid for the exact same SKU in 2024. It’s also $270 more than I paid for it in May 2025. And if you’re thinking you’ll just buy a smaller one, Best Buy’s 8TB Black Friday special is $299.99. That’s $37.50/TB and fifty bucks more than my 20TB cost two years ago. Best Buy’s EasyStore listings
The jump gets sillier when you price a whole NAS. Four of those 20TB drives would have been $999.96 at my 2024 price. This week they’d be $2,199.96. That’s another $1,200 before you’ve bought a case, a board or a single stick of RAM. And the price per usable terabyte only gets worse once you set aside a drive for parity.
If I can wish for one thing in 2027, it would be for hardware pricing to return to reasonable levels. I fear it may take a lot longer than that, though. So making do with less is going to be the new normal for a while. And in that creative constraint, maybe we’ll have some fun along the way instead.

====================================================================================================

Shucking is homelab speak for taking a perfectly good external drive out of its USB enclosure and sticking the SATA drive in your server. At $12.50/TB it was a fantastically affordable way to get massive amounts of storage. At $27.50/TB, absolutely not.
I dug through my receipts
In November 2018 I bought five 10TB EasyStores for $179.99 each. At the time I wrote about doing the rounds of my local Best Buys to pickup more drives on Black Friday as quantities were limited at each location (My 2018 server build log).
October 2023 was my best haul on a per-TB basis. Three 18TB drives put 54TB in my hands for $599.97. This year’s “deal” is fifty bucks shy of that entire order. Best Buy was still advertising those 18TB drives for $199.99 in November 2023. November 2023 deal archive
What about ServerPartDeals?
Buying refurbished or recertified enterprise drives used to be an easy way to bring the cost down. In August 2024 I bought four recertified 14TB Ultrastars from ServerPartDeals for $529.96 before tax. That was 56TB at $9.46/TB, even cheaper than the EasyStores and no shucking to do.
The sad truth is that across the board prices are up a minimum of 58%, and sometimes as much as 218% in just a year or two.
How we got down to three HDD makers
When the EasyStore is that bad, my first instinct is to shop around. Maybe I should try another retailer (to be clear I’m not blaming BestBuy for any of this), buy an enterprise drive, see what’s available recertified. But follow those listings back to the companies that actually make the drives and the choices narrow pretty quickly. There are three big HDD makers left. How did we end up with so few?
Maxtor bought Quantum’s hard drive business in 2001, then Seagate bought Maxtor in 2006. Hitachi combined IBM’s disk business with its own to form HGST in 2003, and Toshiba bought Fujitsu’s HDD business in 2009. Seagate bought Samsung’s HDD business in December 2011, and Western Digital bought HGST in March 2012. The FTC required WD to sell desktop-drive manufacturing assets to Toshiba to keep a third supplier in that market. That left Seagate, Western Digital and Toshiba.
The 2011 Thailand floods hit while those last two deals were underway. WD halted production at all its Thai plants, and its average hard drive selling price went from $45 in fiscal 2011 to $62 in fiscal 2012. There was also a price-fixing case further up the supply chain. NHK Spring pleaded guilty in 2019 to fixing prices for the suspension assemblies that hold the read/write heads, and the court approved a $28.5 million fine. WD’s 2012 annual report · Ninth Circuit opinion
Market collusion and cartel pricing
It’s hard to have anything positive to say in this post. The HDD manufacturing market is so consolidated nowadays - just like the memory market which is equally screwed - that it’s hard to see this as anything but mass market collusion by the HDD manufacturing cartel.
Three companies is an awfully short list when you need to buy a hard drive. And with 89% of WD’s revenue coming from its cloud business in fiscal 2026, I don’t reckon those of us buying a couple of drives for a home server have much impact here. Just like with Nvidia and Micron, consumer markets just aren’t valuable enough so we have to make do with the crumbs instead.
As of November 26, 2026, the 20TB is 2.2× what I paid for the exact same SKU in 2024. It’s also $270 more than I paid for it in May 2025. And if you’re thinking you’ll just buy a smaller one, Best Buy’s 8TB Black Friday special is $299.99. That’s $37.50/TB and fifty bucks more than my 20TB cost two years ago. Best Buy’s EasyStore listings
The jump gets sillier when you price a whole NAS. Four of those 20TB drives would have been $999.96 at my 2024 price. This week they’d be $2,199.96. That’s another $1,200 before you’ve bought a case, a board or a single stick of RAM. And the price per usable terabyte only gets worse once you set aside a drive for parity.
If I can wish for one thing in 2027, it would be for hardware pricing to return to reasonable levels. I fear it may take a lot longer than that, though. So making do with less is going to be the new normal for a while. And in that creative constraint, maybe we’ll have some fun along the way instead.

ESO statement on approval of launch of one million space data centres
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
00:03:44 UTC
09:03:44 KST

Press Release
ESO statement on approval of launch of one million space data centres
26 November 2026
On 25 November, the Federal Communications Commission (FCC), a US federal agency, announced that it has authorised SpaceX to deploy a constellation of one million satellites for data centres in space.
“As approved, this project would not only have profound consequences for astronomy, but also fundamentally alter the night sky for everyone on the planet. We demonstrated these impacts in an extensive technical analysis, and raised them in a response to the US FCC, as did other organisations worldwide. This approval highlights the urgent need for international mechanisms capable of assessing activities whose impacts extend far beyond national borders and affect a global common good,” said Andreas Kaufer, Director General at the European Southern Observatory (ESO).
A peer-reviewed technical analysis [1], led by ESO expert Olivier Hainaut and published earlier this year, quantified the impacts of launching large and bright satellite constellations into space on observations of the night sky. Planned constellations, including SpaceX’s data centres, would hinder humankind’s ability to observe faint cosmic targets, like far-away galaxies, some Earth-like planets around other stars, and even asteroids potentially dangerous to Earth.
While satellite companies, including SpaceX, are making their individual satellites less bright, the sheer number of satellites approved in these projects means that the total number of streaks in images will go beyond what can be mitigated. Beyond optical astronomy, one million satellites would generate radio noise, impacting radio astronomy, pollute the atmosphere from the multiple launches and satellite reentry, and crowd low-Earth orbit, risking major chain collisions.
ESO supports international governance frameworks that balance innovation in space with the protection of the night sky, scientific research, and the long-term sustainability of the space environment. As space activities expand rapidly, decisions with global consequences should be informed by scientific evidence and assessed through appropriate international mechanisms.
Notes
[1] Earlier this year, when the FCC invited public comment on the SpaceX and Reflect Orbital proposals, ESO submitted a response based on this technical study, outlining the threats to astronomy, in collaboration with the UK's Royal Astronomical Society and the International Astronomical Union.
Links
- AAS statement: https://aas.org/press/spacex-data-center-fcc-approval

Beatrice  Kioko (for legal questions)

Bárbara Ferreira (to arrange interviews with ESO’s leadership; general press queries)

Garching bei München, Germany

Email: press@eso.org
Our use of Cookies
We use cookies that are essential for accessing our websites and using our services. We also use cookies to analyse, measure and improve our websites’ performance, to enable content sharing via social media and to display media content hosted on third-party platforms.
ESO Cookies Policy
The European Organisation for Astronomical Research in the Southern Hemisphere (ESO) is the pre-eminent intergovernmental science and technology organisation in astronomy. It carries out an ambitious programme focused on the design, construction and operation of powerful ground-based observing facilities for astronomy.
This Cookies Policy is intended to provide clarity by outlining the cookies used on the ESO public websites, their functions, the options you have for controlling them, and the ways you can contact us for additional details.
What are cookies?
Cookies are small pieces of data stored on your device by websites you visit. They serve various purposes, such as remembering login credentials and preferences and enhance your browsing experience.
Categories of cookies we use
Essential cookies (always active): These cookies are strictly necessary for the proper functioning of our website. Without these cookies, the website cannot operate correctly, and certain services, such as logging in or accessing secure areas, may not be available; because they are essential for the website’s operation, they cannot be disabled.
Functional Cookies: These cookies enhance your browsing experience by enabling additional features and personalization, such as remembering your preferences and settings. While not strictly necessary for the website to function, they improve usability and convenience; these cookies are only placed if you provide your consent.
Analytics cookies: These cookies collect information about how visitors interact with our website, such as which pages are visited most often and how users navigate the site. This data helps us improve website performance, optimize content, and enhance the user experience; these cookies are only placed if you provide your consent. We use the following analytics cookies.
Matomo Cookies:
This website uses Matomo (formerly Piwik), an open source software which enables the statistical analysis of website visits. Matomo uses cookies (text files) which are saved on your computer and which allow us to analyze how you use our website. The website user information generated by the cookies will only be saved on the servers of our IT Department. We use this information to analyze www.eso.org visits and to prepare reports on website activities. These data will not be disclosed to third parties.
On behalf of ESO, Matomo will use this information for the purpose of evaluating your use of the website, compiling reports on website activity and providing other services relating to website activity and internet usage.
Matomo cookies settings:
Additional Third-party cookies on ESO websites: some of our pages display content from external providers, e.g. YouTube.
Such third-party services are outside of ESO control and may, at any time, change their terms of service, use of cookies, etc.
YouTube: Some videos on the ESO website are embedded from ESO’s official YouTube channel. We have enabled YouTube’s privacy-enhanced mode, meaning that no cookies are set unless the user actively clicks on the video to play it. Additionally, in this mode, YouTube does not store any personally identifiable cookie data for embedded video playbacks. For more details, please refer to YouTube’s embedding videos information page.
Cookies can also be classified based on the following elements.
Regarding the domain, there are:
- First-party cookies, set by the website you are currently visiting. They are stored by the same domain that you are browsing and are used to enhance your experience on that site;
- Third-party cookies, set by a domain other than the one you are currently visiting.
As for their duration, cookies can be:
- Browser-session cookies, which are deleted when the user closes the browser;
- Stored cookies, which stay on the user's device for a predetermined period of time.
How to manage cookies
Cookie settings: You can modify your cookie choices for the ESO webpages at any time by clicking on the link Cookie settings at the bottom of any page.
In your browser: If you wish to delete cookies or instruct your browser to delete or block cookies by default, please visit the help pages of your browser:
Please be aware that if you delete or decline cookies, certain functionalities of our website may be not be available and your browsing experience may be affected.
You can set most browsers to prevent any cookies being placed on your device, but you may then have to manually adjust some preferences every time you visit a site/page. And some services and functionalities may not work properly at all (e.g. profile logging-in, shop check out).
Updates to the ESO Cookies Policy
The ESO Cookies Policy may be subject to future updates, which will be made available on this page.
Additional information
For any queries related to cookies, please contact: pdprATesoDOTorg.
As ESO public webpages are managed by our Department of Communication, your questions will be dealt with the support of the said Department.

AI agents now control part of DeepMind's compute for Gemini experiments
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
00:03:18 UTC
09:03:18 KST

AI agents now control part of Deepmind's compute for Gemini experiments, but recursive self-improvement remains distant
Google Deepmind lets AI agents run experiments on their own for the next Gemini model. On the company's podcast, VP of Research Koray Kavukcuoglu, who leads Gemini development, frames this as part of a strategic shift at the lab. Rather than running separate teams for specialized projects like AlphaFold and nuclear fusion, Deepmind now relies on a single general-purpose algorithm for both science and products, he says. Each Gemini generation, he adds, is a hand-built one-off rather than a fixed architecture, while parallel teams work on its successors.
According to Kavukcuoglu, AI agents control part of the compute themselves to run experiments, though the lab is still far from full recursive self-improvement. As a safety measure, Deepmind doesn't reward the agents' internal thought streams ("Chain of Thought") during training, he says, so their intentions stay unfiltered and reviewable in natural language before they act.
Kavukcuoglu also points to limits. Today's AI can depict physical processes such as fluid flows and collisions with visual accuracy, he says, but doesn't understand them precisely. No test can show when models will close those gaps and reach artificial general intelligence (AGI), he argues, because no benchmark captures the core of intelligence. In his view, AGI won't begin until useful everyday products earn enough trust for people to hand decisions over to them, the way they would when working with other people.

NYT Warns Photographers Not To Update Photoshop Over AI Features
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

NYT Warns Photographers Not To Update Photoshop Over AI Features 54
			
		 	
				The New York Times is instructing freelance photographers not to use Adobe Photoshop 2027 (version 28.0.0 and subsequent updates), warning that its new AI features operate "in ways that may not be apparent to users" and "can result in metadata identifying photographs as AI-generated." The Verge reports:  It's not always clear which tools in Adobe's apps are AI-powered, and even some older options like automated selection tools may now be considered part of the AI suite. This can lead to photos ending up with metadata indicating they were edited with AI, which is a blunt way of describing what happened in post-production. For a newsroom like the Times, where photo editing is kept to a minimum, the metadata risks losing readers' trust.
Last year, the Times said that it does not "retouch or digitally manipulate" photos but may "make minimal adjustments to color to ensure we are accurately reproducing the image." In August, Adobe began rolling out support for C2PA metadata across its apps, which indicates whether an image is AI-generated or edited using AI features. The NYT is issuing its restriction to keep these tags from inadvertently appearing in its photos.
